Siemens Energy · ENR-DE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Siemens Energy

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 49,634 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment is unusually favourable for the operating model and unusually hostile for the valuation, and the Consumer must keep the two apart:

  • For the model. Gas and grid demand is structurally higher and contracted for about two to five years. Pricing has stepped up and the step-up is still being earned through backlog conversion. Competitive intensity is low until peer capacity lands in 2027 to 2030. Input-cost pressure is real but mostly contractually passed through.
  • The external factors most likely to move FY2027 to FY2028 numbers:
  • Currency translation, for reported revenue.
  • Labour and component bottlenecks, for conversion speed.
  • A grid price-cost squeeze on FY2028-to-FY2029 deliveries.
  • Not order intake.
  • For the valuation. A risk-off, rising-yield, AI-financing-stress regime has de-rated the whole power equipment group. Siemens Energy fits the profile the market is selling: several moving parts, broken momentum, AI-linked.

The cleanest framing for later sets is that the external forces make the 18-month earnings forecast more predictable than usual, while making the multiple less predictable than usual.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0270C-0546C-0738C-0950C-3562C-3942
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

A701st of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Competitive intensity is low in gas and grid, and is drifting slowly upwards through capacity additions that are dated, mostly 2027 to 2030. It is rising in onshore wind, where Chinese and Nordex pressure is increasing. It is easing in offshore wind (GE Vernova has exited). The net direction over the next six months is roughly stable and benign. Over 18 to 36 months it rises gently, led by grid.

Reasoning:

  • Gas.
  • A three-player oligopoly holds 85% of the market: Mitsubishi Heavy (MHI), GE Vernova (GEV) and Siemens Energy.
  • All three are sold out. GEV is the only one adding capacity aggressively: from 16 GW to 24 GW, now 30 GW by 2030.
  • Investors read GEV's expansion as a 2030s oversupply risk (Redburn's Toyne). It does not bite before 2029 to 2030 because order books run to then.
  • Siemens Energy is deliberately not adding greenfield gas capacity. Its large-unit output goes from 35 to 50 units in FY2027 and medium units from 50 to about 80, by brownfield productivity.
  • Pricing gap: GEV averages about $800/kW against Siemens Energy's $572/kW (Morgan Stanley), and Q3 showed flat sequential price per kW at Siemens Energy (mix) while GEV showed a step-up. That is a sign Siemens Energy competes partly on price in the US, not a sign of a price war.
  • Grid.
  • Capacity is being added by every incumbent:
    • Hitachi Energy: $1.5bn in the US, including a $528m transformer plant doubling its US capacity by 2029.
    • Eaton: $340m to $1.5bn.
    • GEV: bought Prolec.
    • Siemens Energy: +50% transformer and switchgear capacity 2026 to 2030.
  • Korean makers hold 19% of US transformer imports, and Korean and Asian makers are winning northern European transformer tenders on price.
  • Grid is where intensity rises first, around 2027 to 2029. The US executive order of late August 2026, which bars 69 kV-plus equipment from vendors tied to 24 countries (Department of Energy rules due 24 December 2026), lengthens the window in the US.
  • Wind.
  • Chinese turbines are 20% to 40% cheaper. Goldwind is localising in Brazil and targeting 5% share ex-China.
  • Nordex has taken a 48% share of European onshore in 2025 and quintupled US orders.
  • Vestas regained offshore traction with the V236 (UK Vanguard East and West, 2.76 GW).
  • Offshore is a duopoly after GEV's exit, which reduces aggressive bidding.

Interplay: Capacity additions by peers are the balancing loop that will end the pricing window. The timing (2027 to 2030) sits after most of the current backlog is priced, so it affects FY2029-and-beyond margins on new orders more than FY2027 to FY2028 reported margins.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C+465th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: It is a modest, manageable headwind. Costs are rising: steel +32% year to date, copper +18%, aluminium +10%, Brent at about $111. Availability is tight in a few chokepoints: hot-section castings, grain-oriented electrical steel, transformer bushings and skilled labour. Supplier concentration is high and supplier bargaining power is rising.

Contract protection limits the profit effect in FY2027 to FY2028:

  • Price-escalation clauses.
  • 75% to 100% hedging of net transaction exposure.
  • 95% local sourcing.
  • Service contracts indexed 80% to factory consumer prices and 20% to wages, with one-way upward commodity adjusters.

The real exposure is a price-cost squeeze on grid orders executed in FY2028 to FY2029, if transformer pricing has plateaued while copper keeps rising.

Reasoning:

  • Castings. Turbine blades and vanes come from a few suppliers: Howmet, PCC and Yingliu. Yingliu is Siemens Energy's sole Chinese H-class blade supplier and is adding three vacuum furnaces. Siemens Energy is vertically integrating blades and vanes because services need about 70% of them, and it bought CIC.
  • Transformer materials.
  • Grain-oriented electrical steel is about 20% of transformer cost, with very few qualified producers.
  • Copper is 10% to 15% of transformer cost. Raw materials are about 40% of transformer cost overall.
  • Bushings and insulators are the binding small-component bottleneck. Copper supply was de-risked via ASTA Energy.
  • Rare earths. China is about 95% of permanent magnets, which bear on wind. Siemens Energy is diversifying.
  • Supplier relationships are deteriorating. Payable days are down to 45 (bottom 5% of peers) and sourcing directors report "transactional allocation behaviour".
  • The forward risk. Ambit finds per-MVA transformer pricing flat (in India) despite copper +45% to 50% year on year, creating gross-margin compression risk for grid orders executed in FY2028 to FY2029. Pass-through formulas are not disclosed, so pass-through efficiency cannot be modelled from public data.

Model implication: Assume price-cost neutral to slightly negative on grid from FY2028. Assume no material effect on gas services, which carry indexation. Assume a small drag on wind fixed-price legacy contracts.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

D3813th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: The macro force at work is the market regime: risk-off, a rising discount rate, and credit stress at the financing end of the AI build-out. It bites hardest at the valuation level of the sector and the company: the AI-power "picks and shovels" multiple. It bites next at the customer-financing level (developers, neoclouds, offshore wind). It bites least on Siemens Energy's FY2027 to FY2028 earnings, which are backlog-locked.

Reasoning:

  • The regime (week of 23 October 2026).
  • The US 10-year is about 5.10%, with a Fed hike in December over 90% priced. Brent is $111.
  • Credit spreads on neocloud and hyperscaler debt are widening. The Goldman Sachs risk-appetite indicator is negative.
  • European defensives lead cyclicals on all windows. French and Italian sovereign stress.
  • Our environment tilts: Sector Neutral, Fragility Double Underweight, Momentum Underweight. Siemens Energy has several moving parts and a broken price trend (technical D), so it sits on the wrong side of both stock-level tilts.
  • Sector level.
  • Electrical equipment relative strength is graded F (from B and D).
  • Siemens Energy is -12.1% over six months to 13 October and -32% to 23 October.
  • The pattern fits our Wisdom Library entry "a capital-intensive theme cracks at its financing before its demand": suppliers keep beating while the most levered intermediaries re-price.
  • Customer level. High rates lower developer returns (offshore wind returns are down 100 to 300bps against 2021) and delay final investment decisions. Industrial capex has historically shown little sensitivity to rates when inflation is expected to persist (Deutsche Bank).
  • Company earnings.
  • Transaction FX is 75% to 100% hedged. A 10% US dollar move is about €21m.
  • Translation is a material drag on reported revenue (-560bps in Q2 FY2026; -200bps in FY2025) but not on margins.
  • Net cash of about €8bn makes higher rates a small positive through interest income.

Skew: Over twelve months the skew is negative for the multiple and neutral for earnings.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–50

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Five other dynamics matter:

  1. Positive. The offshore wind duopoly after GEV's exit. It improves bid discipline for Siemens Gamesa's largest remaining profit pool.
  2. Negative. Currency translation. The US is 22% of revenue and the Americas 31%. A weaker dollar dilutes reported growth by 50 to 560bps a quarter, and EUR/USD forecasts span 1.10 to 1.25.
  3. Negative. Labour scarcity for high-voltage engineers and commissioning crews. This is the next binding constraint ("bottleneck migration") and caps conversion speed.
  4. Two-sided. The activist and portfolio dynamic. Ananym Capital is pushing for a wind separation. Private equity bidders (CVC, EQT, Bain, Brookfield, KKR) are circling Transformation of Industry. This creates value-crystallisation events.
  5. Negative for the multiple. Peer read-across from GE Vernova, the US bellwether. GEV's capacity raise was read as negative, and its pricing step-up set a comparison Siemens Energy did not match in Q3.

Reasoning: The currency effect is large enough to matter for reported revenue modelling. Over the first nine months of FY2026, reported revenue growth was +9.7% against comparable growth in the teens. The Consumer should model comparable growth and then a separate translation line. The wind duopoly matters more to the multiple (wind is valued at about 8x EBITDA by Huatai against 30x for Gas Services) than to group earnings.

C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

B624th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The threat is low to moderate in the horizon.

Entrant type Who Threat
Adjacent Reciprocating-engine makers (Wärtsilä, Caterpillar), aeroderivative and modular turbine makers (BorgWarner TurboCell at a 100% price premium for speed), fuel cells, power electronics firms in 800-volt direct current (ABB, GEV, startups) Moderate in data-centre behind-the-meter power. Low in utility-scale
Standard Korean, Indian and Chinese transformer and switchgear makers; Chinese wind makers abroad; Chinese cable makers Moderate in transformers outside the US fence; high in onshore wind in emerging markets
Orthogonal SpaceX's in-house blade and vane foundry; hyperscalers self-supplying power Low. Captive use, about 2.8 years to capability

Reasoning: Hot-section casting technology, reference fleets and transmission operator prequalification are slow-to-copy barriers. Entrants come in where products are standard (medium-voltage switchgear, smaller transformers, onshore turbines) or where speed matters more than efficiency (data-centre bridging power). The most relevant entry threat for FY2029 onwards is engines and modular turbines taking a share of the data-centre gas pillar if behind-the-meter turns out to be a bridge.

C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

D4213th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Suppliers are a source of risk, not a support. Bargaining power is shifting toward the scarcest upstream inputs, and the relationship quality is deteriorating.

Reasoning:

  • Castings.
  • Howmet and PCC enjoy strong bargaining power; blades are the industry bottleneck.
  • Yingliu is a single point of dependence for Chinese H-class blades. It targets 34.5% to 38.8% gross margins in FY2027.
  • Siemens Energy's response is in-house blades and vanes, dual sourcing and long-term framework agreements into the 2030s.
  • Wind. Winergy (gearboxes) and blade makers (TPI losses) were part of the quality crisis.
  • Grid. Grain-oriented steel, bushings and composite insulators are under "severe stress". Insulated-gate bipolar transistor (IGBT) and silicon carbide shortages delay grid inverters.
  • Behaviour. Payable days fell to 45 (bottom 5% of peers): Siemens Energy is paying suppliers faster to secure supply. Sourcing directors describe "transactional allocation behaviour" and deteriorating relationships.
  • For the model:
  • Assume upstream cost escalation partly absorbed in margin on fixed-price legacy contracts.
  • Assume conversion delays as a key risk to the FY2027 revenue phasing.
  • Assume no working-capital benefit from payables.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0180C-0358C-0462C-0882C-3276C-3763C-4370
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

B+802nd of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Demand is strong and rising in two of the four businesses (gas and grid), with two to five years of contracted visibility. That visibility comes from sold-out factory slots, not from forecasts, so it counts as a cyclical swing with well over two years of visibility. Grid demand is mostly structural: a replacement cycle, electrification and grid-connection backlogs. Gas demand is a structural step-up (data centres, coal-to-gas, load growth) carrying a cyclical over-ordering element that may peak in FY2026 to FY2027. Wind demand is weak and lumpy.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • Gas turbines.
  • Management raised its market view from 100 GW a year (CMD, November 2025) to 110 to 120 GW a year through 2030. Deutsche Bank adds 10 to 20 GW of data-centre upside.
  • Data centres are a "distinct 40 GW pillar" and about half of North American gas orders.
  • Siemens Energy's commitments (firm backlog plus slot reservations) rose from 87 GW to 95 GW in Q3, against a 90 to 100 GW target. 12 of the 15 GW booked in Q3 converted from reservation agreements, which validates their quality.
  • Lead times are about four years, with slots booked to 2029 to 2030. Commitments of 95 GW against annual delivery capacity in the mid-teens of GW (sector digest: 15 to 16 GW) represent roughly five to six years of output.
  • The key implication: even Morgan Stanley's 2027 bear scenario (global orders halving to 50 GW) shortens backlog duration and weakens new-slot pricing and prepayments. It barely touches FY2027 to FY2029 gas equipment revenue.
  • Grid.
  • Q3 orders were €5.4bn (+28%) with a book-to-bill of 1.48. The segment backlog is €51bn, about 3.6 years of FY2026 revenue.
  • Transmission and distribution equipment demand is growing 10% to 15% a year in 2026 to 2027.
  • Substations are projected to grow from $73bn (2025) to $129bn (2035), about 6% a year. US utility capex budgets are +22% year on year, with transmission +25%.
  • Siemens Energy itself expects a 10% transformer supply deficit to persist in 2030, even after all announced capacity additions.
  • The first soft signal: Q3 grid orders missed consensus by 3% to 5%. Excluding the prior quarter's large HVDC bookings, they showed "no underlying sequential improvement" (Morgan Stanley). This is a plateau at a high level, not a decline.
  • Wind.
  • Q3 orders fell 77% to €1.05bn (book-to-bill 0.4) with no large offshore award; awards shifted to FY2027.
  • Onshore Europe grows about 4% a year. The ex-China pipeline grows about 9% a year to 76 GW by 2030.
  • US offshore is dead and US onshore incentives sunset at end-2027.
  • Transformation of Industry. Q3 orders +32% organic on compression. Cyclical, and leaving the group.

What it means for the model: Group book-to-bill was 1.7 for the first nine months of FY2026 (orders €53.3bn, revenue €31.4bn). Revenue growth for FY2027 to FY2028 is capacity- and price-limited, not demand-limited. Demand risk should be modelled as a FY2029-and-beyond growth and pricing question and as a cash-flow question, not as FY2027 revenue risk.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

C-588th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: No disruption force threatens the 18-month earnings. Three are worth tracking, and on balance they act slightly in the company's favour today, with a modest negative skew after 2028:

  • Behind-the-meter, distributed power for data centres. Net positive for gas turbines now, but it opens the door to reciprocating engines and aeroderivatives.
  • 800-volt direct-current data-centre architecture and solid-state transformers. Mildly negative for conventional transformers inside data centres, positive for medium-voltage content. Revenue starts late 2027 to 2028.
  • Captive or alternative hot-section supply (the SpaceX foundry). Low threat.

The "energy transition kills gas" thesis is being disproved for this decade. The 110 to 120 GW market view was raised, not cut.

Reasoning:

  • Data centres facing five- to seven-year grid queues buy their own power.
  • Behind-the-meter projects are 22% of data-centre capacity under construction and 40% of the pipeline (Bernstein).
  • This lifts gas turbine demand and pricing power, because the buyer pays for the delivery date.
  • The same force favours modular fast-to-deploy options: Wärtsilä dual-fuel engines, Caterpillar, BorgWarner turbo-generators at a 100% price premium for speed, and fuel cells.
  • Jefferies flags reciprocating-engine capacity additions as a threat to large-frame pricing power.
  • The question the market will ask in 2027 is whether on-site gas was a bridge or permanent.
  • 800-volt direct current and solid-state transformers.
  • GEV delivers its first solid-state transformer to a hyperscaler in autumn 2026. ABB's are orderable in the second half of 2027.
  • This redirects data-centre electrical spend toward power electronics. Siemens Energy's grid business is mostly transmission-level, so exposure is limited.
  • SpaceX casting foundry. About 2.8 years to capability, for its own data centres only, with no verified commercial orders (Jefferies, Deutsche Bank).
  • Hydrogen. Slow, subsidy-dependent. Not a driver in this horizon.
  • Battery storage plus solar. Battery costs of $75 to $85/kWh and a 38% compound annual growth rate in US storage make it the main long-run substitute for incremental gas peaking demand. It is a 2028-and-beyond question.
  • Grid-forming, high-voltage direct current (HVDC) and SF6-free switchgear. These are disruption in the company's favour: technology-led share gains for qualified incumbents.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

B624th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: A lot of regulatory change is under way, and it is net positive for grid and gas, net negative for wind. The most consequential recent items:

  • The US executive order fencing out equipment tied to 24 countries (a fence that extends grid pricing).
  • The EU F-Gas SF6 ban (a regulation-dated, margin-accretive upgrade cycle).
  • Ratepayer-affordability politics in the US, a new and growing risk to the pace of utility capex.
  • German grid and EEG reform, a negative for wind developers.

Reasoning:

  • Positive.
  • EU F-Gas Regulation 2024/573: SF6 is banned in new medium-voltage switchgear up to 24 kV from 1 January 2026, in 52 to 145 kV from 2028, with high-voltage phase-out by 2032. SF6-free kit is 10% to 15% dearer. National Grid has approved only three suppliers, Siemens among them.
  • EU €584bn grid plan to 2030. The Net-Zero Industry Act (40% EU-made clean tech by 2030).
  • The US SPARK initiative and the bipartisan Senate permitting draft, under which data centres over 20 MW pay their own grid costs.
  • India Green Energy Corridor III, Italy's €23bn contract-for-difference scheme, Australia's Capacity Investment Scheme.
  • The US fence: Department of Energy rules due 24 December 2026 bar 69 kV-plus equipment from vendors tied to 24 countries.
  • Negative or risk.
  • US: the Ratepayer Protection Act passed the House 417 to 3 (dedicated tariffs for loads over 100 MW). "Affordability" now outranks "data centres" on utility calls. FY2026 utility capex growth was revised down to 23% from 25% (Bernstein).
  • Section 232 tariffs cost about €40m of margin on the wind backlog.
  • US wind incentives sunset at end-2027. US offshore wind is effectively zero.
  • Germany: the draft EEG and grid package shifts connection and curtailment risk to developers; 17 lenders warned of financing risk. Siemens Energy's backlog is not retroactively affected.
  • Company-specific.
  • Exit from the German state guarantees (2025), replaced by a €9bn bank guarantee facility. This removed the dividend ban and the bonus restrictions.

Fragility: The regulatory support for grid is legislated and dated, so it has low fragility. The data-centre support depends on utility commissions and politics, so it is medium fragility, rising into the 2026 US mid-term cycle.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

B+823rd of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Strongly. Three sector drivers are accelerating demand at once, and they compound:

  1. Load growth from AI data centres and electrification. A distinct 40 GW gas pillar. Utilities' generation capex is +36% year on year.
  2. The grid replacement and reinforcement cycle. Half of installed transformers reach retirement age by 2040. The EU grid plan is €584bn. Over 1,500 GW of capacity sits in grid queues.
  3. Coal-to-gas switching and fleet replacement.

The acceleration shows in Siemens Energy's orders (Gas Services orders +61% in Q3, record), in pricing (+20% on gas, +5% to 12% a year on grid), and in management raising the gas market view from 100 GW to 110 to 120 GW.

Reasoning:

  • What is distinctive is the conversion of load growth into price rather than only volume, because supply is capacity-bound until 2028 to 2030.
  • The driver's sensitivity sits in data centres (25% to 30% of the Gas Services backlog; half of North American gas orders).
  • Siemens Energy books slots only for permitted sites, and reservation fees are non-refundable. This lowers cancellation risk.
  • The driver is at peak acceleration now. Its second derivative probably turns negative in 2027:
  • Tougher order comparisons.
  • Utility capex growth revised down (25% to 23%).
  • AI financing stress.
  • The level of demand stays high through 2030 on company and peer evidence.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

A762nd of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Customer demand strongly supports the 18-month thesis and is contractually hard to reverse. The weak links are hyperscaler and developer financing and ratepayer politics, which make support for orders booked after 2027 moderately fragile.

Reasoning:

  • Behaviour now. Customers are:
  • Booking slots up to four years ahead through multi-project framework agreements.
  • Paying reservation fees and advance payments.
  • Accepting escalation clauses and punitive termination penalties.
  • Paying premiums for delivery dates.
  • Absorbing 25% to 45% price rises by cutting their own workforces rather than pressing suppliers (an expert).
  • Data-centre customers are also bypassing grid queues (a 150% surge in private-network grid orders, per an engineering consultancy).
  • The customer base:
  • Grid: over 2,000 customers.
  • Gas: hyperscalers and private developers are 25% to 30% of Gas Services backlog demand, and half of North American gas orders.
  • Gas services: 17-year agreements; over 70% of buyers take long-term service.
  • Fragility points:
  • Data-centre funding (neocloud credit spreads widening).
  • Affordability backlash at US utilities (capex growth revised down to 23%).
  • Wind developers' economics (high rates, German reform, US policy).
  • Morgan Stanley's "over-ordering" scenario (2027 orders -50%).
  • Mitigants:
  • Siemens Energy books only permitted data-centre sites.
  • Reservation fees are non-refundable.
  • Cancellation clauses exist.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

B+633rd of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Regulators broadly help (see C-04). The help is most robust where it is legislated and dated, and most fragile where it depends on US utility commissions and politics.

Helps:

  • EU F-Gas SF6 dates.
  • The EU grid action plan (€584bn).
  • National transmission plans: UK RIIO-T3 (about £70bn of National Grid investment), Italy's contract-for-difference scheme, India Green Energy Corridor III.
  • The US equipment fence and the Department of Energy rules due 24 December 2026.
  • The permitting reform drafts.

Hurts:

  • The US wind incentive sunset (end-2027) and Section 232 tariffs.
  • German reforms shifting risk to wind developers.
  • The affordability politics behind the Ratepayer Protection Act.
  • Spain's draft decree on data-centre renewable matching (€9bn of investment at risk).

Fragility:

  • Low for grid (multi-year regulated capex with weighted-cost-of-capital indexation).
  • Medium for data-centre-driven gas and grid (permit brakes in Texas, Virginia, Spain; US mid-term politics).
  • High for wind.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

C706th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: The realistic addressable markets are large relative to Siemens Energy, but each is supply-limited now and slower-growing after 2028.

  • Gas turbines. 110 to 120 GW a year to 2030; at Siemens Energy's about €440m per GW, that is roughly €50bn to €55bn of new-unit value a year, plus a services pool on a growing fleet. Siemens Energy's gas equipment revenue is a fraction of that.
  • Grid. About $300bn broadly in 2025. Substations grow from $73bn (2025) to $129bn (2035) at about 6% a year. Transmission and distribution equipment demand grows 10% to 15% a year in 2026 to 2027. Siemens Energy's Grid revenue (€14bn in FY2026) is about 5% of the broad market.
  • Wind. About $146bn in 2025. The ex-China pipeline grows about 9% a year to 76 GW in 2030. Europe onshore grows about 4% a year.

Implication for mid-term growth:

  • Volume growth can run at about 6% to 10% a year in grid and low single digits in gas units after capacity additions.
  • On top of that sit price (about 5% to 10% a year now, flattening) and service growth (high single to low double digits).
  • So the market allows about 12% to 15% revenue growth to FY2028, set by capacity plus price.
  • It allows about 7% to 10% a year from FY2029 to FY2031 as price stops rising and volume becomes the driver.

Reasoning:

  • Consensus revenue growth is +15.0% in FY2027, +13.0% in FY2028 and +10.9% in FY2029.
  • That fits the capacity plus price path to FY2028 and looks full for FY2029 unless the gas market holds above 110 GW and the grid deficit persists.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0662C-1068
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C627th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: The company is in the middle of its revenue and margin upcycle and near the top of its order cycle in gas. The three cycles are out of phase, and that is the most important structural fact for the model:

  • Orders: probably peaking in gas in FY2026 (Q3 orders +61%, "not expected to be repeated every quarter") and plateauing in grid at about €5bn to €7bn a quarter.
  • Revenue: accelerating, and capacity-limited. Comparable growth was 12.8% in FY2024, 15.2% in FY2025 and 18.5% in Q3 FY2026.
  • Margins: rising, as the old backlog is replaced by repriced orders. Grid backlog margins have risen about 300bps a year for consecutive years.

Revenue and margins will keep rising through FY2028 even if orders turn down in FY2027. The cycle is not turning in the reported numbers. It may be turning in orders.

Reasoning:

  • Margin before special items moved: FY2023 -8.9%, FY2024 1.0%, FY2025 6.0%, 9M FY2026 12.6%, Q3 14.2%.
  • Backlog moved from €138bn (FY2025) to €162bn (Q3 FY2026).
  • The ordering pattern (sold-out slots, reservation fees, slot reservation agreements) is classic late-upcycle behaviour. Customers front-load to secure slots.
  • The sector digest: "price rises are done, the earnings rise is not." The price plateau "at a high level" means two to three more years of rising executed margin as pre-2022 contracts run off.
  • Wind is at an order trough and an early margin recovery. Three years of losses ended with Q3's €75m profit.

Turning signals to watch:

  • Gas Services book-to-bill below 1.0.
  • The gas market below 100 GW a year.
  • Grid book-to-bill below 1.2.
  • Quarterly working-capital outflow above €1bn (prepayment reversal).
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

B684th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: The external picture is one of strong tailwinds at value-chain and industry level, colliding with a strong headwind at the level of market valuation and financing. The tailwinds are the stronger force for earnings through FY2028. The headwinds are the stronger force for the share price over the next six months.

Driver by driver (sector digest themes):

Theme Strength Siemens Energy exposure Net
"The sold-out slot becomes contracted margin" Strongest Grid half and gas Tailwind. Margins rise through 2028 even if prices plateau
"Speed to power" (behind-the-meter data-centre power) Big Gas turbines Tailwind through 2028. The bridge-or-permanent question is a 2027 risk
Law-dated distribution grid (SF6, smart grid) Early, slow Blue portfolio; qualified-supplier status Mild tailwind
800-volt direct current Early Limited; transmission-level Neutral to mildly negative after 2028
Automation engines Not relevant None None
  • Industry level.
  • Grid queues, a 57 GW US power shortfall to 2028, utility capex +22% and the US fence: tailwinds.
  • Peer capacity additions dated 2027 to 2029, the affordability backlash, Chinese wind: headwinds.
  • Value-chain level.
  • Pricing power sits with capacity owners and suppliers of scarce castings and steel.
  • Siemens Energy is a capacity owner, which is a tailwind, but is squeezed by concentrated upstream suppliers, a moderate headwind.
  • Market level. Risk-off, 5.1% US 10-year, AI financing stress, sector relative strength at F: strong headwind to the multiple.

Overall: The external change forces support an operating model of mid-teens revenue growth and rising margins through FY2028. Demand-side risk belongs in FY2029 onwards, in the free-cash-flow line and in the multiple, not in FY2027 revenue.

Internal change forces/risks

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The internal change is among the strongest and broadest in European capital goods. A management team that survived a near-failure of the group in 2023 has used the external supercycle well: it priced up, refused speculative capacity, cleaned the balance sheet, restarted returns and began simplifying the portfolio.

For modelling, three implications:

  1. Formal guidance is a floor. The record since FY2024 is consistent and large beat-and-raise.
  2. FY2027 to FY2028 will carry portfolio discontinuities (Transformation of Industry, India, royalty) and one-off separation costs that the Consumer must model explicitly.
  3. Share count falls about 3% a year while orders exceed revenue, because buybacks are funded partly by prepayments.

The two weaknesses:

  • Wind is still being fixed rather than exited.
  • Governance and pay choices lean management-friendly. Both deserve a modest discount but do not change the direction.

Internal risks

Group judgement

Risks

C+425th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is bimodal:

  • Fundamental risk to FY2027 to FY2028 earnings is modest. Earnings are backlog-locked, margin-led and the company has net cash. The wind warranty tail is the main exception.
  • Risk to free cash flow is larger: the prepayment reversal if orders drop below revenue.
  • Risk to the share price is high: a crowded AI-power proxy in a risk-off, high-yield regime, with an order-peak narrative forming and a binary event on 11 November.

The trough analysis says about 20% to 35% downside remains in a bad case, after a 38% fall from the high. The Consumer should carry three scenario levers into the model:

  1. Wind margin and charges.
  2. Gas order intake and its effect on prepayments and free cash flow.
  3. Grid price-cost on FY2028-to-FY2029 deliveries.

Revenue scenarios for FY2027 can be narrow.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

A721st of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Standards are improving substantially in commercial and financial discipline, and more slowly in operational quality, where wind remains the weak point.

Evidence of improvement:

  • Commercial terms. Stricter terms and conditions, risk-sharing, escalation clauses, consequential-loss limits, reservation fees and selective bidding. New orders come in above backlog margin. The order book is booked only against permitted data-centre sites.
  • Cost.
  • Gamesa integration savings of €300m.
  • Onshore wind factories cut from 10 to 4.
  • SG&A target of 8.7% of revenue by FY2028.
  • Brand royalty removed via Omterra.
  • Guarantee fees (about €500m over three years) ended by the early exit from state guarantees.
  • Governance and controls.
  • Gamesa's board was dissolved (July 2024) and its finance functions integrated.
  • COSO risk and control framework.
  • Shareholder representatives 90% independent. KPMG, clean opinion.
  • Delivery. All FY2025 targets met or exceeded. Wind reached a quarterly profit in Q3 FY2026.

Where standards lag:

  • Wind quality:
  • Legacy 4.X and 5.X platform defects.
  • A Taiwan turbine fire in August 2026.
  • About €2bn of unprofitable legacy contracts.
  • €2,474m warranty provisions and €984m onerous-contract provisions at September 2025.
  • Customer perception: data-centre developers call Siemens Energy "transactional", and utilities see Hitachi as faster and cheaper.
  • Expert observers still describe bureaucratic approval processes.

For the model: The commercial-discipline improvement is the mechanism behind backlog margin expansion. It is durable while capacity is scarce. Operational quality in wind is the variable that can still produce negative surprises.

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

  • The balance sheet (€8bn net cash) means none threatens solvency. They threaten EPS by 5% to 20% and free cash flow by more.
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

A661st of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: Concentrated, and in a way that favours predictability for 18 months and disfavours it after. The forecasts differ from FY2020 to FY2025 on three lines (revenue growth about 11% a year against 7.3%; EBITA margin 12% to 18% against an average near zero with a -10.6% trough; free cash flow €5bn to €7bn against €0.2bn to €1.0bn). Two correlated drivers generate about 70% of the profit difference: conversion of the gas and grid backlog at repriced margins (volume on the backlog at old margins is about 46% of that, margin uplift about 54%). The removal of wind losses is about 18%, and portfolio and self-help items (Transformation of Industry growth, India, the royalty) about 11%. In cash, one variable, the sign of orders minus revenue, generates the whole difference from history. The drivers are few, but the dominant one is contractually underpinned and observable, which is the best kind of concentration for a short horizon. The dangerous concentration is in the margin half of the gas and grid driver, which history cannot calibrate and which carries all of the FY2029 and beyond risk.

The decomposition (EUR billions; my estimates of FY2025 segment profits, since the company's FY2025 segment detail is not in the files):

Driver FY2025 to FY2030 EBITA change (base case €2.36bn to €12.06bn, +€9.7bn) Share FY2026 to FY2028 (+€3.8bn) Share
Gas Services (12.07 at about 12.7% to 22.52 at 22.5%) +3.5 36% +1.5 39%
Grid Technologies (11.05 at about 14.5% to 23.83 at 21.0%) +3.4 35% +1.5 39%
Siemens Gamesa (about -1.2 to +0.56) +1.8 18% +0.4 9%
Transformation of Industry, India, royalty, corporate +1.0 11% +0.5 13%

Inside the gas and grid €6.9bn: volume at FY2025 margins about €3.2bn; margin uplift (price, mix, absorption) about €3.8bn. Of the margin uplift, about €0.6bn to €0.8bn over FY2026 to FY2028 is fixed-cost absorption that reverses quickly in a downturn (P-01); the rest is contracted price and mix.

Reasoning:

  • Why this concentration helps for 18 months. The dominant driver has five sub-conditions, all observable each quarter: (1) units shipped against the 50 and 80 to 100 capacity plans; (2) the booked price converting (the pre-close confirmed it); (3) no cancellations or deferrals (76% of the gas turbine backlog is with regulated utilities, 24% with data-centre developers, Morgan Stanley 26 October; management books slots only against permitted sites and reservation fees are non-refundable; Morgan Stanley's stress test puts a 50% delay in 2028 data-centre deliveries at about €1bn of revenue and €0.3bn of EBITA, about 3%); (4) cost overruns absent (indexation per D-01 point d); (5) wind not producing a charge. Against that stand genuinely diffuse foundations: more than 2,000 grid customers, 17-year service contracts attached to over 70% of turbines, over 90 countries. The structure is "one factor, many contracts", the opposite of a single-customer or single-product concentration.
  • Why the two segments are one factor, not two. Gas and grid share the demand driver (power load and AI), the regional pricing premium (the US), the prepayment mechanics and the FY2027 execution calendar. They differ on supply constraints (castings against electrical steel and bushings) and on competitive timing (GE Vernova's 30 GW against Hitachi's 2029 Mississippi plant). For the downside inside the window, treat them as one driver with two supply chains; for FY2029 onwards, as one driver with one variable, the price of orders booked from 2027.
  • Where the concentration hurts. The €3.8bn margin uplift has no history to calibrate it: the company's best pre-2026 margin was 6.0%, and the FY2023 trough was driven by charges, not volume. The precedents for the reverse (Siemens Power and Gas 16% to about 5% from 2014 to 2018; GE Power's margin halving in 2017; 2010 to 2013 high-voltage substation prices down up to 20%) show that when the single variable turns, it turns with a one- to two-year lag after orders and with price falling on top of volume. That is after the window, but the market will price it inside the window (D-07).
  • Cash is a one-variable line. Free cash flow FY2027 to FY2030 differs from FY2020 to FY2025 by about €5bn a year, of which about €5bn is EBITDA and the rest nets to roughly zero (higher capital spending and cash tax against lower interest and special items). The working-capital flow, +€3.6bn in FY2026 to -€1.5bn in FY2029 in the base case, is set by one thing: whether contract liabilities (now €28.1bn) keep growing. That is why the cash cone is wide (D-04) even though the profit cone is narrow.
  • The sell-side's own dispersion locates the uncertainty in the same place. FY2028 revenue estimates span €54.9bn to €59.2bn (±4% around the median); EBITDA €10.0bn to €11.6bn (±8%); EPS €6.56 to €9.50 (±18%). The spread widens as one moves down the income statement, which says the market disagrees about margin conversion (gas equipment margins, grid pricing in 2028, wind), not about volume. Our FY2028 EPS of €8.36 sits at the median.
  • Peers, for calibration. GE Vernova's drivers are the same two (gas backlog repricing, electrification capacity) with a higher US share and a slot-reservation share of 56% against Siemens Energy's 31%, so its cash is more concentrated on reservation fees and its margins more on US pricing. ABB's and Schneider's profit changes are diffuse (short-cycle products, mix, software) and their multiples sit at the 73rd and 77th percentiles of their ten-year ranges against Siemens Energy's 32nd: the market pays for diffuse drivers and discounts concentrated ones, which is the fair-multiple point for the memo's topic #11.

For the Consumer: monitor the one driver through its five observable sub-conditions and the one cash variable through quarterly contract liabilities. Do not spend monitoring effort on the diffuse foundations; they are not where the forecasts can go wrong in 18 months.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

A742nd of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The strategy has changed decisively. Over 2024 to 2026 it moved from a "growth journey" broad energy conglomerate toward a "highly disciplined execution machine" focused on power generation and transmission. The priorities are:

  • Backlog margin quality over volume.
  • Brownfield productivity over greenfield capacity.
  • A €6bn capex programme for gas and grid capacity.
  • Portfolio simplification: Transformation of Industry out, Omterra brand, Siemens Energy India in.
  • Up to €10bn of shareholder returns by FY2028.

The change is well articulated and well resourced: €6bn capex (about 20% above the prior cycle), +7,000 net employees in two years, about €8bn net cash, and a €9bn guarantee facility.

Reasoning:

  • The choices are coherent with the cycle:
  • Refusing greenfield gas capacity protects against the 2030s oversupply that GEV's expansion invites.
  • Grid capacity +50% by 2030 still leaves a stated 10% transformer deficit, so pricing is protected.
  • Selectivity in wind accepts weak orders to stop loss-making intake.
  • The trade-off the Consumer should model: Siemens Energy is choosing price and margin over share. Expect slower gas volume growth than GEV but higher incremental margins, and wind revenue growth near zero.
  • The weaker part: wind strategy is still "fix, then decide". The answer to the activist's spin-off demand is "execute the plan first", so the group carries wind risk for at least 18 more months.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

A722nd of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Meaningfully, with the main steps dated:

  • Transformation of Industry.
  • €5.7bn FY2025 revenue (about 13% of the group), 17,000 staff, a low-teens margin.
  • Being legally and operationally separated. The intent is full deconsolidation while keeping a minority stake.
  • No structural decision before mid-calendar 2027.
  • Goldman Sachs is running a sale of a majority stake in the steam turbine business, with private equity bidders named.
  • Valued above €10bn (Jefferies: €13bn enterprise value at 12x FY2030 EBIT).
  • Onshore wind. India and Sri Lanka onshore wind divested. Onshore factories cut from 10 to 4. Product variants reduced. Selective bidding.
  • Brand. Omterra replaces the licensed Siemens brand from late 2026.

A wind exit is not on the table yet. That is the largest remaining perimeter decision, and it is conditional on onshore stabilising.

Reasoning and model consequences:

  • The Transformation of Industry exit lifts the group margin by at least about 50bps mechanically (Deutsche Bank). It removes about 12% to 13% of revenue and roughly 10% of profit.
  • Proceeds above €10bn would probably fund further buybacks or the Siemens Energy India purchase.
  • The Consumer must choose a modelling convention:
  • Either keep Transformation of Industry consolidated until a deal is signed, which is probably what most consensus models do.
  • Or show it as discontinued from the second half of FY2027 or FY2028, which lowers group revenue by about €6bn to €7bn, lowers EBIT by about €0.8bn to €0.9bn, raises the margin, and adds cash or a minority stake.
  • Stranded corporate costs should be assumed: ABB's precedent after its Robotics sale was about $25m a quarter.
  • Ambit flags a 9% to 10% value-destruction risk for Siemens Energy India minorities. That is a reputational and related-party issue, not a group-profit issue.
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

D5512th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Modestly and sensibly. Expansion is mostly deeper penetration of adjacent segments in existing markets, not new territory:

  • US manufacturing. Charlotte transformer plant expansion (early 2027) and a new US switchgear plant, aligned with the US fence and tariffs.
  • The data-centre power segment. Gas turbines behind the meter, grid connections, and a stated ambition for Grid to exceed €20bn revenue beyond 2028.
  • India. Taking a 51% controlling stake in Siemens Energy India, about €4.5bn, fully consolidated by June 2028. That business had a 23.6% operating margin in its latest period (Ambit).
  • Hydrogen. Electrolysers and digital twins (GalpH2Park). CCUS. SF6-free "Blue" portfolio.

Reasoning:

  • Each move follows demand the company is already supply-constrained against, so execution risk is lower than for a true new-market entry.
  • India is the most material: it buys control of a high-margin, high-growth franchise at a full price, from the former parent's sphere.
  • Hydrogen is a cost line with option value, not a profit driver in the horizon.
  • The weakness: the company is not building scale in the fastest-growing data-centre niches (modular power, 800-volt direct current, liquid cooling) where ABB, Schneider and Eaton are positioning. That is acceptable given its transmission focus.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

A821st of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The price rises are real, large and well above cost inflation. They are already partly visible in reported margins, with a larger part still latent in the backlog. They are now plateauing at a high level rather than still rising.

Evidence of application:

  • Gas.
  • Like-for-like large gas turbine pricing is up more than 20% year on year.
  • New orders are about 20% above backlog pricing, at about €440m per GW (Deutsche Bank).
  • CCB finds industry pricing up about 50% per kW, from about $2,000 to $3,000 per kW at plant level.
  • Gas Services margin rose from 13.0% to 17.3% year on year in Q3, "driven by service and pricing".
  • Grid.
  • Procurement pricing +10% to 12% year on year. Transformers +8% to 12% a year, and +75% to 90% against 2020.
  • Grid margin rose from 15.9% to 19.9%.
  • Backlog margin has risen about 300bps a year for consecutive years (Morgan Stanley).
  • Customers pay premiums for earlier slots.
  • Services. Compounding escalators (80% factory consumer price index, 20% wages), one-way upward commodity adjusters, 17-year average contract length.

Against real-ness:

  • Gas pricing per kW was flat sequentially in Q3 (mix). GEV still prices about 40% higher per kW than Siemens Energy.
  • Grid pricing is "plateauing on a high level".
  • Ambit: Indian per-MVA transformer prices are flat despite copper +45% to 50%.

Model consequence: No further price increases are needed for margins to keep rising through FY2028. The repricing is in the backlog and converts as old contracts ship. The risk is price erosion on new orders after 2028, not reversal of prices already booked.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

C+556th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: M&A is modest in count but one deal is material: Siemens Energy India, a 51% stake for an estimated €4.5bn, fully consolidated by June 2028. The rest are small vertical integrations (CIC castings; an ASTA Energy stake for copper). Acquisitions were €105m in FY2026 to date.

Reasoning:

  • India is strategically sensible. It gives control of a 23.6%-margin franchise in a high-growth market and removes a structural ownership anomaly: Siemens AG about 59%, Siemens Energy 11% to 16%. That anomaly creates conflict in the Transformation of Industry separation.
  • Discipline is unproven.
  • The price is set within the Siemens family.
  • Ambit warns of related-party pricing and minority friction.
  • The track record is poor. The 2022 buyout of the Siemens Gamesa minorities (about €4bn, completed early 2023; outside the files) preceded the June 2023 quality crisis and €2.2bn of quality charges, a clear value-destructive deal.
  • Integration since 2024 has improved:
  • Gamesa's functions absorbed.
  • Gamesa's board dissolved.
  • €300m savings on track.
  • Management's stated preference is capacity capex and buybacks over large M&A.

For the model:

  • Include the India consolidation from the date control passes (by June 2028). It adds revenue, a high margin, minority interests, and a cash outflow of about €4.5bn.
  • Do not assume further large M&A.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

A761st of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Sharply stepping up, from a standing start.

What has happened:

  • The dividend restarted at €0.70 for FY2025 (about €600m) after the state-guarantee ban ended. Policy is a 40% to 60% payout of net income.
  • A €6bn buyback programme runs to FY2028:
  • About €2.8bn executed by end-July 2026.
  • A third tranche of up to €2bn runs from 24 September 2026 to 31 March 2027.
  • FY2026 shareholder returns are about €3.6bn (€0.6bn dividend plus €3.0bn buybacks).
  • The CMD framed up to €10bn of returns by FY2028.

What is mooted: Morgan Stanley expects annual buybacks to rise to about €3bn at the 11 November results, and Deutsche Bank sees ample room. Transformation of Industry proceeds (above €10bn) would add capacity.

Reasoning:

  • Net cash was €8.1bn at Q3 FY2026 (FactSet), with €11.1bn of cash and €3.0bn of debt.
  • Consensus free cash flow for FY2026 to FY2028 sums to about €21bn.
  • After €6bn of capex is deducted, about €4.5bn for India and dividends of about €1.6bn to €2.9bn a year, the group can fund €3bn a year of buybacks and stay net cash.
  • Caution: some of that cash is customer prepayments (€22.3bn of contract liabilities). Management's net-cash buffer partly exists to absorb a prepayment reversal. Buyback capacity is therefore larger while orders exceed revenue than after.
  • For EPS: at about €120 a share, €3bn a year retires about 25m shares (about 3% a year). Consensus already implies a falling share count (about 834m in FY2026 and 811m in FY2028, against 855m basic in FY2025).
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–66

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Four further positive forces:

  1. Omterra rebrand removes the Siemens royalty. Jefferies puts it at €300m to €400m a year, about 90bps of margin; the annual report shows €51m for FY2024. Even the low figure is pure margin. The high figure would be a material upgrade not clearly in consensus.
  2. Service annuity compounding. The installed base grows with every turbine shipped. Deutsche Bank estimates 160 GW of additions by 2030 could add €3.6bn of annual service revenue within the decade.
  3. Wind legacy wash-out. About €2bn of unprofitable contracts and warranty remediation run off. Each quarter of clean execution lowers the quality cash drain (€450m a year of quality-related cash outflow in FY2025).
  4. Interest income on €8bn to €11bn of cash, and the end of guarantee fees.

Plausible but not yet present: a wind separation (activist-driven, conditional), and a US listing or index re-rating as the group becomes comparable to GEV.

Reasoning: Items 1 to 4 are mechanical, "gravitational" improvements that need no heroic execution. They argue for leaning FY2027 to FY2028 margin estimates toward the upper half of ranges. The royalty discrepancy is worth resolving, because it is worth either about 0.1pp or about 0.8pp of margin.

C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

B584th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: There is moderate scope, and it points toward more portfolio simplification and higher capital returns, not toward a change of core strategy.

  • Ananym Capital has publicly demanded a strategic review and a spin-off of Siemens Gamesa. The board "welcomed constructive input" but chose to execute the wind plan first. Jefferies reads the Transformation of Industry review as raising the odds of a wind spin-off once onshore stabilises.
  • Siemens AG (about 10%) is a passive and declining holder. Its residual leverage is the trademark licence, which it can cancel if a competitor holds over 15%; that leverage fades once Omterra is in place.
  • The German state no longer has influence after the guarantee exit.

Reasoning: No controlling shareholder exists. The board sets direction, chaired by Joe Kaeser (ex-Siemens AG CEO), with 50% employee representatives under co-determination. Co-determination slows radical restructuring (for example, Spanish wind job cuts were negotiated down from 423 to 255). A wind spin-off is the main lever an activist could pull. It is plausible in 2027 to 2028 if wind posts a few profitable quarters, and it would be strongly positive for the multiple because wind is the discount driver.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

A742nd of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Strong, broad and well-resourced. This is a genuine "massive corporate change" pattern:

  • Strategy refocused to power generation and transmission.
  • Pricing discipline.
  • Capacity investment.
  • Portfolio exit (Transformation of Industry).
  • Brand independence.
  • Restored incentives.
  • A capital return programme of up to €10bn.
  • A balance sheet that moved from a state rescue (2023) to about €8bn net cash (2026).

The changes are funded by customers (prepayments) and by the earnings step-up, not by leverage.

Reasoning:

  • The positive changes act on all four value levers at once:
  • Margin: pricing, wind repair, royalty, Transformation of Industry exit.
  • Growth: capacity.
  • Capital: buybacks.
  • Multiple: simplification, comparability with GEV.
  • The limiting factor is bandwidth:
  • 2027 stacks a carve-out, a rebrand, an Indian consolidation, two capacity ramps and wind repair.
  • It does so under a CFO and Grid head whose contracts end in November 2027.
  • The negative offsets: wind and compensation optics. They are real but smaller in economic weight than the positive changes.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

B+703rd of 19

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: A well-populated staircase. The first step (11 November 2026) is the most important and the most binary. Later steps are mostly well-founded mechanical deliveries plus two genuinely novel portfolio events (the Transformation of Industry deal and a possible wind separation).

Next 6 months (to April 2027):

Reasoning:

  • The staircase is mostly delivery steps of an already-told story. The market knows about pricing, capacity and margin targets. Novelty is concentrated in:
  • The FY2030 number.
  • Capital returns.
  • The Transformation of Industry price.
  • A wind exit.
  • Positive novelty after 11 November depends on portfolio events, not on operating news.
  • Against the staircase stands the negative narrative staircase: peak orders (from Q4 FY2026 order comparisons), GE Vernova capacity, and the AI-financing stress.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

B+663rd of 19

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

B+663rd of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structure in gas turbines and high-voltage grid is as supportive as it gets in capital goods: a concentrated oligopoly, sold-out capacity, high technical and regulatory barriers, a growing installed base, and customers paying for delivery dates. Siemens Energy is a top-three player in each and number one in offshore wind.

The support has three known weak points:

  1. Time. Scarcity is the source of excess margin, and competitor capacity dated 2027 to 2030 will erode it on new orders.
  2. Upstream. Concentrated casting and electrical-steel suppliers and labour capture part of the rent.
  3. Wind. Siemens Gamesa sits in a structurally worse industry: Chinese cost leaders, policy-dependent demand, and quality-driven share loss.

For modelling:

  • The industry supports rising reported margins through FY2028 with high confidence.
  • It supports flat-to-higher pricing on new orders through about 2028 with medium confidence.
  • After that, it implies margin mean-reversion pressure on new orders, which shows up in reported numbers from about FY2030.

The empirical precedents fit a late-upcycle conversion story with a cash-flow false-friend tail.

3a

Value chain dynamics

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?A762nd of 19full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C625th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The route to market is mostly direct (utilities, transmission system operators, independent power producers, hyperscalers, engineering, procurement and construction contractors), and intermediaries matter little. That is neutral to mildly positive and not fragile.

Reasoning:

  • Large gas turbines, HVDC and transformers are sold direct through tenders and framework agreements. Services are sold direct through 17-year contracts.
  • Partners add reach: Jereh in turbine distribution, EPC partners on turnkey.
  • Turnkey engineering, procurement and construction is the low-margin, risky stage. Siemens Energy participates only selectively, which limits margin leakage.
  • Mild hindrances:
  • Developers complain about rigid bidding.
  • Siemens Energy is seen as "transactional".
  • In onshore wind, developers distrust legacy warranty terms, which slows commercial discussions.
  • The channel's fragility lies in customer goodwill, not structure. It matters when capacity frees up after 2028 and customers can choose.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

B+643rd of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Competitor behaviour helps on balance today:

  • GEV exited offshore wind and leads gas pricing upwards.
  • Hitachi and others add grid capacity only against demand.
  • Western wind makers price for margin.

It hurts in three places:

  • Vestas and Nordex take wind share.
  • GEV's capacity expansion seeds a 2030s gas oversupply worry that weighs on the multiple now.
  • Hitachi, ABB and Korean makers win some grid business on lead times and price.

Siemens Energy's position is robust in gas and grid and fragile in onshore wind.

Reasoning:

  • GEV is the price setter and capacity swing producer in gas: its Q2 2026 orders were 20 GW, 18 GW of them slot reservations. Siemens Energy's preference for firm orders over reservations is a differentiating discipline.
  • The gas pricing gap with GEV ($572/kW against $800/kW) is partly mix (US share) and partly a signal of lower pricing power.
  • In grid, Hitachi Energy's 15% margin target for FY2028, against Siemens Energy's 19.9% in Q3, suggests Siemens Energy is pricing at or above peers.
  • Fragility: low in gas and grid until 2028; high in onshore wind, where every lost tender lowers fixed-cost absorption at Siemens Gamesa.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

B+603rd of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The forces act mainly on Siemens Energy's customers and suppliers, not on its core products. They leave its position robust for the next two to three years and moderately exposed after.

Actor How the force acts Effect on Siemens Energy
Demand: data centres Grid queues of five to ten years push behind-the-meter gas and private networks; 800-volt direct current redesigns the data-centre electrical room Positive now; mixed after 2028
Demand: utilities Load growth plus the affordability backlash; generation capex +36%, transmission +25% Positive, with political fragility
Demand: wind developers Higher rates, policy reversals, curtailment risk Negative for Siemens Gamesa
Competition GEV capacity; Asian transformers; Chinese wind Mildly negative, dated 2027 to 2030
Regulators SF6 ban; the US fence; permitting reform Positive

Industry structure
