C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B764th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Siemens Energy is number two or three in each of its profit-making businesses, and close to number one. It is number one in offshore wind and sub-scale in onshore wind.

Business Rank and share Gap to leader
Large gas turbines #2 by orders, 26% (Jefferies) vs GEV 30%; or #3 in 2025 combined-cycle orders, 28% vs MHI 36% and GEV 33% (Bernstein) A few points. Took 42% of global orders above 100 MW in Q3 FY2026 (Huatai)
High-voltage grid Top three with Hitachi Energy and GEV; about 20% of global power transformers, about 30% of HVDC systems (Jefferies) Hitachi Energy is the leader (about 18% of transformers; backlog over $60bn)
Offshore wind #1 incumbent: half to two-thirds of European installed turbines Duopoly with Vestas, which is regaining share with the V236
Onshore wind Not top five globally (all Chinese); behind Vestas (about 30% ex-China) and Nordex (16% ex-China; 48% of European onshore in 2025) Far behind

Reasoning: Rank matters because in gas and grid the top three are the only qualified suppliers for the largest projects. Each of the three is capacity-constrained, so share in 2026 to 2028 is set by capacity and selectivity rather than by competitiveness. Siemens Energy deliberately accepts a slightly lower gas share in exchange for price.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

B804th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Very concentrated where Siemens Energy earns its profit.

  • Large gas turbines. The top three hold about 85%. The share distribution is extremely steep: numbers four to ten are niche or domestic Chinese makers, with under 15% combined.
  • HVDC systems. Concentrated in three or four Western suppliers (Hitachi, Siemens Energy, GEV), with about 30% for Siemens Energy.
  • Large power transformers. Less concentrated. The top five probably hold well above 30%, but a long tail of Korean, Chinese, Indian and Spanish makers (for example Arteche, Hyosung, HD Hyundai) holds meaningful share.
  • Ex-China wind. The top three Western makers hold 80% to 90%. Global wind is dominated by Chinese makers (about 67% of 2025 installations; all of the top five).

Reasoning: The gas and HVDC concentration is protected by technology (hot-section casting, decades of reference fleets) and by prequalification. Transformers are the most contestable piece, and that is where price competition from Korean and Asian makers appears first. For the model, gas pricing is the most defensible line and transformer pricing the least.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

B724th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Shares are very stable in gas turbines and HVDC, moderately stable in transformers, and unstable in wind.

  • Gas turbines. Shares have shifted only by a few points among the three incumbents over decades. New entrants have taken essentially nothing outside China. SpaceX's foundry is captive and two-plus years away.
  • Transformers. Shares have moved modestly toward Korean and other Asian makers (Korean makers: 19% of US transformer imports), and toward specialists in medium voltage. The US fence now freezes entry from 24 countries.
  • Wind. Large shifts:
  • Chinese makers went from domestic-only to large overseas order intake (49.4 GW of overseas orders in 2024 to 2025).
  • Within the West, Siemens Gamesa lost onshore share to Vestas and Nordex after the 2023 quality crisis.
  • Nordex rose to a 48% share of European onshore in 2025.

Reasoning: Stability in gas supports a long-dated service annuity: every turbine sold locks in 17 years of service. Instability in wind is why the market values Siemens Gamesa at about 8x EBITDA.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

C+665th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: In gas and grid, share consolidates toward the qualified, capacity-owning incumbents, and Siemens Energy is among them. In wind it consolidates toward the most reliable Western makers (Vestas, Nordex) and toward China, and Siemens Gamesa is on the losing side onshore.

Reasoning:

  • Gas.
  • Siemens Energy and GEV combined will control about 60 GW of capacity by 2030, about 70% of global demand (Jefferies).
  • Utilities consolidate spend with tier-one suppliers to secure capacity.
  • The emerging counter-force is modular, fast-to-deploy alternatives for data centres. Some incremental share goes to engine and aeroderivative makers.
  • Grid.
  • Prequalification creates a "preferred supplier" structure: National Grid approves only three suppliers for SF6-free equipment.
  • Customers increasingly sign multi-year framework agreements with tier-one suppliers.
  • Hitachi is seen as more competitive on lead times and price. ABB has a 5% to 10% lead-time edge in some product lines.
  • Advantage, not size, is the sorting rule. The most advantaged are those with scarce capacity, qualified designs and installed bases. Siemens Energy has all three in gas and grid, and not in onshore wind.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

B+683rd of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Today every major gas and grid competitor puts margin and returns first, and behaviour is benign. But this is a cyclical attitude, not a structural one. In the last gas downcycle (about 2017 to 2020; outside the files: GE Power's collapse and Siemens Power and Gas restructuring), industry prices did fall when volume fell, and margins collapsed. In wind, Western makers have switched to "value over volume" after the 2018 to 2022 price wars. Chinese makers are volume-first abroad, despite a domestic self-discipline pact.

Reasoning:

  • Gas.
  • All three suppliers use reservation fees, firm-order conversion and price escalators.
  • GEV books 20 points of higher pricing and leads on price.
  • Siemens Energy prices below GEV ($572/kW against $800/kW) but is not discounting into the market.
  • Grid.
  • Incumbents add capacity only against secured demand. Siemens Energy's own guidance leaves a 10% deficit in 2030.
  • Korean and Asian makers buy share with lower transformer prices.
  • Wind.
  • Vestas and Nordex say the "hard-learned rule" is profitability over growth.
  • Chinese turbines are 20% to 40% cheaper.
  • Do prices fall when volume falls? Historically yes, with a lag of about one to two years, because fixed-cost heavy factories chase utilisation. That is the mechanism by which a 2027 order slowdown would show up in FY2029 to FY2030 margins.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

B565th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The attitude is stable as long as factories are sold out (through roughly 2028 to 2029) and becomes unstable after that. GEV is the competitor most likely to change behaviour. It has raised its 2030 capacity target twice (from 16 to 24 to 30 GW), leads on price, and has scale and a premium valuation to fund share gains.

A shark attack on gas or grid pricing within 18 months is unlikely: no one has spare capacity to attack with. A shark attack in onshore wind, mainly from Chinese makers in emerging markets, is already under way.

Reasoning:

  • Over the last 18 to 36 months, attitudes improved sharply:
  • Wind makers stopped price wars.
  • Chinese makers signed a self-discipline pact, and domestic bid prices recovered about 10% from the trough.
  • Grid makers moved to framework agreements and indexation.
  • Instability triggers to monitor:
  • Order growth turning negative in 2027.
  • Peer capacity landing in 2028 to 2030: GEV gas 30 GW; Hitachi Mississippi 2029; NKT Karlskrona Q2 2027; Nexans +40% by 2028.
  • Customer financing stress.
  • Weakness at Siemens Energy itself:
  • Wind is the visible weakness competitors already exploit (Vestas in offshore, Nordex onshore).
  • In gas, Siemens Energy's lower price per kW suggests it is the one most exposed if a price war starts in the US.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

B+703rd of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The structure is highly supportive in gas and grid now, with medium fragility. Support rests on three pillars:

  1. Concentration: the top three in gas hold about 85%.
  2. Scarcity: sold-out slots, with lead times of four to five years for large transformers and about four years for turbines.
  3. Barriers: castings, prequalification, installed base, and the US fence.

Pillars one and three are durable. Pillar two, scarcity, is the one generating today's excess margins, and it has a visible expiry: dated competitor capacity additions in 2027 to 2030 and a possible order normalisation from 2027.

Reasoning:

  • The Wisdom Library entry "customers' capital discipline is the asset owners' scarcity" fits: the length of the pricing window is set by build times and the gap between today's price and what new capacity needs.
  • In gas, new capacity needs castings that take years. In grid, factories take two to three years.
  • So the window plausibly extends to about 2029, consistent with the sector digest ("executed margins rise through 2028").
  • The fragility is asymmetric across time:
  • Low for FY2027 to FY2028 reported margins (already in the backlog).
  • Medium to high for the pricing on orders booked in 2027 to 2028, which become FY2030-and-beyond revenue.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?B624th of 19full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

C586th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The substitute threat is moderate and slowly rising for incremental gas demand. It is low for grid, which every alternative needs.

Substitutes for gas turbines:

  • Solar plus storage. Lithium iron phosphate batteries at $75 to $85/kWh; US storage growing at a 38% compound annual growth rate to 279 GWh a year by 2030.
  • Small modular reactors (2030-plus).
  • Demand-side efficiency per GPU.
  • Reciprocating engines.

Substitutes for new grid hardware:

  • Dynamic line rating (unlocks existing capacity).
  • Refurbished transformers (prices +81% since 2021 push buyers to refurbishment).
  • Battery-based balancing replacing some re-conductoring.

Reasoning:

  • None of these displace the 2026 to 2030 gas order book, which rests on firm-power needs of data centres and coal-to-gas switching. Gas demand was revised up, not down.
  • Our risk file's "accelerated transition" scenario (Asian solar and battery substitution collapsing gas orders) is medium plausibility over the long term and low within 18 months.
  • For grid, substitutes slightly slow volumes but do not reduce the need for HVDC, transformers or switchgear. Every renewable and storage project needs connection.

4a

Business quality

B+722nd of 198 answers
Judgement72C-4174C-4274C-4370C-4462C-4578C-4642C-4780
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

B+722nd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Siemens Energy's competitive advantage is real and layered in gas and grid:

  • An installed-base service annuity.
  • Hot-section and HVDC technology.
  • Prequalification.
  • Scale.

On top of that sits a temporary but powerful scarcity window, which is producing today's excess margins.

The most model-relevant insight: the earnings step-up to FY2028 is driven mainly by mechanical forces (backlog repricing, service growth, wind run-off, royalty removal, portfolio mix) rather than new competitive wins. That makes it unusually predictable.

The advantages are weakest where they would matter in a downturn:

  • Customer goodwill (value extracted, not shared).
  • Price leadership against GEV.
  • Onshore wind.

For the Consumer:

  • Model margin expansion through FY2028 with high confidence.
  • Model service growth as the durable floor.
  • Treat pricing on orders booked from 2027 as the variable that decides FY2030-and-beyond margins.
C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

B+743rd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Siemens Energy holds strong, mostly widening moats in gas and grid, and a narrowed moat in wind.

Moat Type Strength Direction
Installed base and service annuity in gas turbines Customer-related (switching costs: original-equipment parts, hot-section know-how, 17-year contracts, over 70% service attachment); about one-sixth of global electricity is generated on its technology Strong Widening. Every unit shipped adds 17 years of service; about 160 GW of additions by 2030
Hot-section technology and casting access Production / knowledge Strong Stable to widening (in-house blades and vanes, CIC, Yingliu qualification)
Prequalification and reference fleets Regulatory and political (transmission operator approvals; SF6-free certification; 30- to 50-year utility procurement cycles; the US fence against 24 countries) Strong Widening (SF6 dates; the US executive order)
Scale in HVDC and large transformers; grid-forming expertise Scale and knowledge (about 30% of HVDC, 20% of transformers; full-stack hardware, software and service) Strong Widening while capacity is scarce; narrowing gradually from 2028 as peer capacity lands
Offshore wind installed base and the SG 14/15 platform Customer and scale Medium Stable (duopoly) but contested by Vestas
Onshore wind None meaningful Weak Narrowed by the quality crisis and share loss

Reasoning:

  • The moats that matter for valuation, the service annuity and prequalification, are the slowest-moving and most durable.
  • The moat that matters most for current excess earnings, capacity scarcity, is the least durable. It is a "first-mover window", not a moat.
  • One acknowledged limitation (a former Mitsubishi Power executive): Siemens Energy's internal conservatism on performance guarantees may cost premium pricing against more aggressive peers.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

B744th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Siemens Energy is one of the three largest power-equipment groups in the world:

  • FY2025 revenue €39.1bn; FY2026 about €44bn.
  • About 100,000 employees, before the 17,000 in Transformation of Industry leave.
  • Order backlog €162bn: comparable to GE Vernova's $176bn, and far above Vestas (€72bn) or Hitachi Energy (over $60bn).

Scale confers:

  • The ability to offer full-stack grid and gas packages.
  • Purchasing power with suppliers, though less than it should be: payable days are only 45.
  • R&D capacity (€1.2bn a year).
  • A balance sheet able to carry multi-year fixed-price risk and €9bn of guarantee lines.
  • A large service fleet.

Reasoning:

  • Scale is necessary to be a qualified supplier of HVDC links and large gas fleets, so it is a barrier to entry.
  • Scale did not protect against the wind quality failure. A large, complex organisation was slower to detect platform defects, as the "big company disease" pattern predicts.
  • Scale relative to GE Vernova (revenue about $38bn) is similar, but GEV has a higher US share and higher gas pricing. Scale alone does not give Siemens Energy pricing leadership.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

C-629th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: In gas turbines and high-voltage grid, Siemens Energy is demonstrably not inferior, and in some niches it leads:

  • HVDC and grid-forming studies.
  • SF6-free switchgear (one of three National Grid-approved suppliers).
  • Offshore wind scale (the most mature 15 MW offshore platform in active deliveries).

The dominant customer utility today is the delivery date, and Siemens Energy has slots.

It is inferior in onshore wind (reliability) and possibly on responsiveness and lead time in parts of grid, where Hitachi and ABB are seen as more competitive.

Evidence against inferiority:

  • Q3 FY2026 share of above-100 MW gas turbine orders of 42%.
  • Grid book-to-bill of 1.48.
  • 12 of 15 GW of gas orders converting from reservation agreements.
  • Customers accepting escalators and termination penalties.

Evidence of possible inferiority:

  • GEV's higher price per kW, which may reflect stronger utility or US positioning.
  • Data-centre developers calling Siemens Energy "transactional".
  • Multinational utilities seeing Hitachi as more competitive on lead time and price.
  • Onshore wind share loss after the 4.X/5.X defects.

Reasoning: The disproof test (the Sophos/Bytes approach) is passed for gas and grid: no evidence that customers systematically avoid Siemens Energy where it has capacity. It is failed for onshore wind.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

A782nd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Strong, applied pricing power in gas and grid, shown in reported numbers. It is plateauing, with early signs of some cost in customer goodwill.

Applied evidence:

  • Gas: like-for-like large turbine pricing +20% year on year; new orders about 20% above backlog. Gas Services margin went from 13.0% to 17.3% year on year on "service and pricing", in a seasonally weak quarter.
  • Grid: procurement pricing +10% to 12%. Margin from 15.9% to 19.9%. Backlog margin +300bps a year (Morgan Stanley).
  • Services: compounding escalators; one-way upward commodity adjusters.
  • Reservation fees and stricter terms are themselves pricing power.
  • Book-to-bill stays high despite the price rises, so the erosion of surplus customer utility is minimal so far.

Limits:

  • Gas price per kW was flat sequentially in Q3.
  • Siemens Energy prices below GEV.
  • An expert estimates scarcity (constrained slots) explains about 40% of grid price increases. That part reverses when slots free up.
  • Customers' goodwill is being drawn down ("transactional").

Reasoning: Under the Wisdom Library's "pricing power" test, a price rise that holds with volume growth is evidence. Here both price and orders rose. Roughly half of the pricing is scarcity-based (cyclical) and half is cost pass-through and technology premium (structural, such as SF6-free or HVDC).

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

D4212th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Little. In a seller's market Siemens Energy is extracting value, not sharing it:

  • Price increases above cost inflation.
  • Reservation fees.
  • Tighter liability terms.
  • Selective bidding.
  • No stated customer-sharing principle.

That maximises near-term margin. It is not a "scale economies shared" franchise, and it builds latent customer resentment that will matter when capacity normalises around 2028 to 2030.

Reasoning:

  • The behaviour is rational at the top of a capacity cycle, and peers do the same.
  • But a Costco-style sharing culture would sustain share in the downturn. Siemens Energy's culture looks more like a disciplined oligopolist's.
  • Evidence of cost: data-centre developers describing it as "transactional" and less responsive; developers facing rigid bidding; utilities preferring Hitachi on lead times.
  • For the model: expect some pricing give-back on new orders once lead times normalise, with share retention relying on the installed base and prequalification rather than goodwill.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

B+802nd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Very strongly through FY2028. This is the most important positive answer in the set.

Margin expansion over the next two to three years comes mainly from mechanical forces that need execution but not heroics:

  1. Backlog repricing. Old contracts ship out and new ones carry about 20% higher gas pricing and higher grid margins; grid backlog margin rises about 300bps a year.
  2. Installed-base growth feeding the 17-year service annuity, at 25% to 35% aftermarket margins.
  3. Wind legacy run-off. About €2bn of unprofitable contracts and €2.47bn of warranty provisions are consumed. The quality cash drain fades.
  4. Removal of the Siemens brand royalty (€51m to €400m a year).
  5. Mechanical mix uplift from deconsolidating Transformation of Industry (at least +50bps).
  6. Interest income and buybacks on a net-cash balance sheet funded by customers.

Reasoning and model use:

  • These forces explain why the CMD segment targets (18% to 20%) were nearly reached two years early.
  • They are why consensus FY2027 to FY2028 margins (14.4% and 16.3%) are credible without assuming new pricing gains.
  • My reconstruction of consensus FY2028 EBIT of €9.3bn:
  • Gas Services about 20.5% on €19.4bn.
  • Grid about 20.5% on €20.0bn.
  • Siemens Gamesa about 4% on €11.6bn.
  • Transformation of Industry about 13.5% on €6.7bn.
  • Reconciliation about -€0.1bn.
  • In words: consensus needs gas and grid only slightly above their CMD ranges and wind at mid-target.
  • Against peers, these forces work in Siemens Energy's favour until about 2028. After that, the gravity reverses on new-order pricing as peer capacity arrives. The service annuity keeps compounding.

The management team is a capable, now well-proven operator team. It took the company from near-failure to a disciplined, cycle-aware strategy that is delivering ahead of plan. It sets conservative targets and raises them on evidence.

Its weaknesses:

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

C+666th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: High ambition on margin and returns. Deliberately moderate ambition on volume and share.

The ambitions:

  • "Execution machine". Group margin 14% to 16% (FY2028), pulled forward one year. Expected 20%-plus for FY2030 (Morgan Stanley: 20% to 22%).
  • Grid Technologies to become a €20bn-plus revenue business beyond 2028.
  • Pure-play electrification.
  • Up to €10bn of returns.

Against peers:

  • GE Vernova is more aggressive on gas capacity (to 30 GW) and on pricing.
  • ABB and Schneider are more aggressive in data-centre product breadth and software.
  • Siemens Energy's ambition is "outcompete on returns", not "outgrow".

Reasoning:

  • The ambition is high relative to the company's own history: margins from -9% (FY2023) to 20% (FY2030).
  • It is calibrated to avoid over-building into a cycle. Bruch refuses greenfield gas capacity despite demand.
  • That is the right ambition for an 18-month to four-year forecast: lower growth upside, lower downside.
  • The missing ambition is a credible plan to make wind a franchise (Vestas-like) rather than a repair job.
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

A722nd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities?

Judgement: A smart, cycle-aware strategy, with the right priorities, moderately bold.

The priorities:

  • Price and selectivity over volume.
  • Brownfield over greenfield.
  • Service annuity growth.
  • Portfolio simplification.
  • Independence from Siemens AG.
  • Capital returns from customer-funded cash.

Why it widens the moat:

  • Every turbine shipped grows the 17-year service base.
  • Prequalification and SF6-free technology deepen grid barriers.
  • Vertical integration of blades and vanes protects service supply.

How it adapts to a plausible range of environments:

  • If the gas boom persists, pricing on remaining slots rises.
  • If it fades, there is no stranded capacity, and the service annuity and grid carry earnings.

Reasoning:

  • The bold parts: separating Transformation of Industry, rebranding away from Siemens, and the timing of taking India control.
  • The not-bold part: wind. Fixing a sub-scale onshore business rather than exiting keeps a drag and a tail risk in the group, and delays the multiple convergence with GE Vernova.
  • A strategic gap against peers: limited presence in software and digital and in data-centre-internal power (800-volt direct current, modular). This is acceptable given the transmission focus.

B704th of 19

Actions:

  • Took over unexpectedly in March 2020.
  • Oversaw the 2022 buyout of the Gamesa minorities (about €4bn; outside the files) just before the June 2023 quality crisis. That led to €2.2bn of quality charges, a €4.6bn net loss in FY2023, and a €12bn state-backed guarantee package.
  • Since then:
  • Stopped 4.X/5.X sales, cut onshore sites from 10 to 4, dissolved Gamesa's board, delivered €300m of savings.
  • Sold 18% of Siemens India (€2.1bn).
  • Exited the state guarantees early.
  • Set and beat the CMD targets.
  • Imposed pricing and selectivity discipline.
  • Refused speculative capacity.
  • Separated Transformation of Industry and launched Omterra.
  • Restarted the dividend and €6bn of buybacks.
  • At Linde Engineering he lifted the margin by 700bps over five years (to above 15%) with capital discipline.
  • Contract extended to April 2030.

Reasoning:

  • On our Wisdom Library operator-quality dimensions he scores high on operational force, decision velocity (Transformation of Industry, rebrand) and communication directness ("the fiscal year 2023 was a bad year for all shareholders").
  • He scores partial on capital allocation (the Gamesa buyout; buybacks at high prices) and on wind-specific expertise (a gas and engineering background).
  • He is not a founder-owner. Ownership comes through guidelines (300% of salary) and programmatic awards.

  • But the board's willingness to reset targets retroactively weakens the discipline of the scheme.
  • The overall evidence is "adequate governance, generous pay".

  • Revenue growth runs about 2x the five-year history. The FY2020 to FY2025 compound annual growth rate was 7.3%; consensus for FY2025 to FY2028 is 13.5%. Revenue is capacity- and price-led and backlog-supported, so the higher rate is credible.
  • The quality is good: it is margin from pricing, mix and repair of losses. It is not from leverage or accounting.
  • FY2029 and beyond depends on whether pricing on orders booked in 2027 to 2028 holds. That is the cyclical exposure.
  • Peers: Siemens Energy's FY2027 to FY2028 EPS growth of 40% and 32% is far above ABB (18%, 15%), Siemens AG (14%, 15%) and Prysmian (28%, 18%).
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

B664th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Scope is high but deliberately bounded:

  • €6bn of capex over FY2026 to FY2028. Consensus capex is €2.2bn, €2.1bn and €1.9bn.
  • Grid capacity +50% by 2030.
  • Gas medium units from 50 to about 80 to 100. Large units from 35 to 50 in FY2027.

Returns on that capital are very high, because slots are pre-sold at elevated prices and customers prepay. But management will not chase greenfield gas capacity, and the high-return window closes as peer capacity arrives around 2028 to 2030.

So high return on new capital is available for about three years, not a decade. That argues for a fair multiple below GE Vernova's (about 35x) and Schneider's: roughly the stock's own ten-year median to "defendable" band of 16.8x to 20.7x 24-month-forward earnings, not a compounder multiple.

Reasoning:

  • Grid Technologies earned a 52% return on invested capital in FY2024 (Bernstein) even before the latest repricing.
  • Brownfield expansions use existing sites and are partly funded by reservation fees.
  • Beyond capex, capital deployment is buybacks (€6bn), India (about €4.5bn) and possibly the Transformation of Industry proceeds coming back.
  • Research and development is falling as a share of revenue (3.5% to 3.1%). That is efficient now but a long-term risk.
C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

C+706th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Returns have gone from negative to very high in two years, because:

  • Profit rose sharply.
  • Customers fund working capital: contract liabilities are €22.3bn, about half of FY2026 revenue.
  • Reported capital employed is therefore small.

Evidence:

  • Group return on invested capital was -4.2% in FY2024, against a peer median of about 11% (Bernstein).
  • Grid return on invested capital was 52% in FY2024.
  • The company's target is a return on capital employed above 15% by FY2028. It looks very conservative given about €7bn to €9bn of EBIT in FY2027 to FY2028 on modest net operating assets (equity of about €12bn to €18bn, minus net cash of about €8bn).

Post-tax return on new invested capital:

  • Incremental EBIT from FY2025 to FY2028 is about +€7.3bn (€2.0bn to €9.3bn).
  • Incremental capital is about €6bn of capex (net of depreciation, about €0.5bn to €1bn a year), plus the €4.5bn India purchase.
  • That gives a post-tax incremental return well above 50%.
  • Caveat: it is mostly price on existing assets, not return on the new capital itself.

Peers: ABB return on capital employed 29.9%, Prysmian 21%, Vestas 11.8%.

Reasoning:

  • Returns are flattered by prepayments. On a gross basis that excludes customer funding, returns would be mid-teens to twenty-plus percent: still good, and more cyclical.
  • Tangible returns are higher than reported returns because of the Gamesa goodwill.
  • For the model: prepayment-funded returns reverse if book-to-bill drops below 1. Treat return on capital as cyclically inflated.
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

C+646th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Conversion is exceptionally high now and cyclically inflated. It will normalise, and the downside to consensus is in timing, not in direction.

History:

Fiscal year FactSet free cash flow Free cash flow / net income Note
FY2024 €1.0bn 0.9x
FY2025 €3.9bn (company pre-tax: €4.7bn) 2.7x
FY2026 — — 9M pre-tax free cash flow €7.16bn; guidance about €8bn pre-tax, which Deutsche Bank calls conservative

Consensus:

Fiscal year Free cash flow Free cash flow / net income
FY2026 €7.6bn 2.0x
FY2027 €6.8bn 1.3x
FY2028 €6.8bn 1.0x

The cumulative FY2026 to FY2028 total of about €21bn matches the CMD's €20bn.

Working-capital mechanics:

  • Contract liabilities (customer advances and reservation fees) of €22.3bn fund inventories (€10.4bn in FY2025) and contract assets.
  • In Q3 FY2026: contract liabilities +€881m, contract assets -€899m, inventories -€521m.
  • FactSet's net working capital measure (receivables plus inventories minus payables) is rising: from 41% to 46% of revenue.
  • Payable days have been compressed to 45.
  • The cash conversion cycle was -78 days in FY2025.

Reasoning:

  • Inflows grow while orders exceed revenue (nine-month book-to-bill 1.7). They flatten when book-to-bill nears 1. They reverse when it drops below 1.
  • Bernstein estimates €3bn to €4bn of cumulative outflow by 2028 as exceptional prepayments normalise.
  • Consensus fading to 1.0x by FY2028 already assumes neutral working capital, not an outflow. If the Bernstein reversal occurs, FY2027 to FY2028 free cash flow could be €1.5bn to €2bn a year below consensus.
  • Precedents: GE Power 2018; the Vestas contract-asset build-up.
  • Watch triggers:
  • A quarterly working-capital outflow above €1bn.
  • Gas Services book-to-bill below 1.0.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

C+556th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Moderately clean, with five things to watch.

  1. Special items. - Company profit before special items for the first nine months was €3,946m, against FactSet adjusted EBIT of €3,577m. The gap is about 9%: restructuring, Siemens Gamesa remediation and purchase-accounting amortisation. - FY2025: profit before special items €2,355m against net income €1,685m (company) or €1,414m (FactSet). - FY2024 net income (€1.19bn) was flattered by a gain on the Siemens India stake sale (€2.1bn proceeds). FY2024 EPS of €1.37 is not a clean base.
  2. One-offs within the adjusted figure. The Q3 FY2026 margin of 14.2% included positive project-related effects and Transformation of Industry timing effects. Cost-to-cost percentage-of-completion accounting can create cumulative catch-ups in either direction.
  3. Provision accounting. The €2,474m warranty and €984m onerous-contract provisions at Siemens Gamesa are management estimates. Releases or top-ups swing reported profit.
  4. Capitalised development. About €184m capitalised against €158m amortised. Neutral.
  5. Tax and leakage. - Reported tax was 32.3% in FY2025 and 25.4% in the first half of FY2026. Consensus implies about 28% including minorities. - Minorities (€473m on the balance sheet) will rise with Siemens Energy India consolidation. - Foreign tax risks are in the mid hundreds of millions of euros.

Positively, the end of guarantee fees (about €500m over three years) and of the brand royalty are clean, recurring improvements.

For the Consumer:

  • Model a 26% to 29% tax rate.
  • Model explicit special items for FY2027: separation, rebrand, wind.
  • Model a growing minority line after India.
  • Use the brokers' basis (FY2025 EPS €1.63) as the like-for-like EPS base.

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

A742nd of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Price is the main source of margin and about 40% of revenue growth through FY2028, then fades. I expect realised price to add about 5pp to revenue growth in FY2027, about 4.5pp in FY2028, about 3pp in FY2029 and about 2pp in FY2030, as the backlog booked at 2024 to 2026 prices replaces the backlog booked before 2022. Net of cost inflation, price adds about 0.8pp to 1.0pp a year to the group margin in FY2027 and FY2028, about 0.4pp in FY2029 and close to zero in FY2030. No further price rises on new orders are needed for this path: the price is already in the backlog. The risks to price are dated and mostly sit after the 18-month window: a price-cost squeeze in grid on FY2028 to FY2029 deliveries, and erosion of scarcity rents on new orders once competitor capacity lands in 2028 to 2030, which reaches reported revenue mainly from FY2030.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • The repricing that is already contracted.
  • Gas: new orders priced about 20% above a year earlier at about €440m per GW (Deutsche Bank, August 2026); the pre-close call of 30 September 2026 confirmed that new orders carry "substantially higher margins" than both the executed backlog and orders signed in early FY2026. Slots are booked to 2029 to 2030, so FY2029 to FY2030 gas equipment revenue is largely priced at today's levels.
  • Grid: procurement pricing +10% to 12% a year; transformers +75% to 90% against 2020; backlog margin rising about 300bps a year (Morgan Stanley); Q3 FY2026 Grid margin 19.9% against 15.9% a year earlier.
  • Services: 17-year average contracts, escalators indexed 80% to factory consumer prices and 20% to wages, compounding, with one-way commodity adjusters; transactional service pricing up by double digits (Jefferies).
  • Why price lifts revenue more than margin. Much of the price rise matches cost inflation (copper +18% and steel +32% this year; wages +4% to +5%), and that part adds revenue at roughly constant margin. Only price above cost reaches the margin. I put realised price at about 6% to 7% on gas and grid equipment, about 4% on services and 0% to 2% on wind in FY2027, against cost inflation of about 3% to 4%.
  • The plateau. Gas price per kW was flat sequentially in Q3 FY2026 (Morgan Stanley; management attributes it to mix and scope) and grid pricing is "plateauing on a high level". A plateau at a high level still means two to three more years of rising executed margin as old contracts run off (sector digest: "price rises are done, the earnings rise is not"). Jefferies expects equipment prices to plateau in 2026 and decline gradually from the mid-2030s; NKT's 2024 to 2026 cable contracts carry twice the revenue per kilometre of 2019 to 2020 ones.
  • How much of the price is scarcity rent. About half: an expert estimates scarcity explains about 40% of grid price rises; the rest is cost pass-through and technology premium (SF6-free switchgear at a 20% premium in medium voltage and 74% to 202% capital-cost premiums at transmission levels in the UK; HVDC in a three-supplier oligopoly). Scarcity rent reverses on new orders when lead times normalise; it does not reverse on orders already in the backlog.
  • The pricing gap with GE Vernova. Siemens Energy's implied gas price is about $572 per kW against GE Vernova's about $800 (Morgan Stanley). Morgan Stanley attributes it mainly to GE Vernova's higher share of US orders, but it also signals that Siemens Energy is the price follower. That matters most if a price war starts in the US after capacity lands; it does not change FY2027 to FY2028.
  • Where price can disappoint inside the forecast window: grid price-cost. Ambit reports flat per-MVA transformer pricing in India despite copper +45% to 50%. Hitachi Energy has about 70% of its grid business on index contracts; Siemens Energy does not disclose its coverage. I assume the grid margin rises only 0.7pp from FY2027 to FY2028 (19.3% to 20.0%) and 0.5pp a year after, below the run-rate of backlog margin gains, to allow for this squeeze.
  • Precedents for when price turns. In 2010 to 2013 high-voltage substation order prices fell up to 20% (Siemens, January 2012), ABB faced 4% to 5% price pressure on orders and Alstom Grid earned 6.2%; in the 2016 to 2020 gas downturn prices and service terms reversed and GE priced aggressively to regain share. In each case prices fell one to two years after volume fell. With gas orders likely peaking in FY2026 and peer capacity landing in 2028 to 2030, new-order price erosion would most likely start in calendar 2028 to 2029 and reach reported revenue from FY2030 to FY2031.

Price contribution in my base case:

Fiscal year Revenue growth of which price of which volume and other Margin effect of price net of cost
FY2027 +13.0% about +5pp about +8pp about +0.9pp
FY2028 +12.4% about +4.5pp about +7pp, and India +0.6pp about +0.8pp
FY2029 +10.5% about +3pp about +6pp, and India +1.5pp about +0.4pp
FY2030 +6.8% about +2pp about +4.5pp about 0pp

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7560P-0174
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

B603rd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Operating leverage is material, but for FY2027 to FY2028 the range of outcomes is narrower than for a typical industrial. Revenue is backlog-locked, so the swing variables are margin outcomes per segment, not volume.

Evidence:

  • Q3 FY2026 incremental margin excluding wind was about 42% (Gas Services 38%, Grid 34%). Consensus incremental margin is about 30% to 36%.
  • Depreciation and amortisation is about €1.8bn to €2.0bn (about 4% of revenue). SG&A is about 9%; research and development 3%.
  • Siemens Gamesa has very high operating leverage around breakeven: €10.6bn to €11.6bn of revenue, so each 1pp of margin is about €110m.

Sensitivities (FY2027 base):

  • 1% of revenue at a 30% to 40% drop-through is about €150m to €200m of EBIT, about 2% to 3% of EPS. Deutsche Bank: 1.7% per 1pp of organic growth.
  • 100bps of group margin is about €500m, about 7% of EPS. Deutsche Bank's stated "9.6% EPS per 50bps" looks overstated; I calculate about 3.5% to 4% per 50bps.

Range for FY2028 EBIT (my reconstruction, against consensus of €9.3bn):

  • Downside €7.9bn (-16%): gas and grid at 18%, wind at 0%.
  • Upside €10.2bn (+9%): gas and grid at 22%, wind at 5%.
  • Wider bear case: a gas order collapse plus a wind warranty charge, without revenue loss before FY2029, takes EBIT to about €7bn to €7.5bn.

Reasoning:

  • The skew of EBIT outcomes is mildly negative relative to consensus because consensus already embeds segment margins above the CMD targets. Upside comes from the royalty, faster pricing and wind.
  • In a true downturn after 2028, operating leverage would bite hard: fixed factories, and price falls with volume.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

A741st of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Operating leverage will lift the margin, but it is the smaller of the two engines and the more reversible one. Over FY2026 to FY2028 I expect EBITA to rise by €3.8bn (from €5.36bn to €9.18bn) on revenue up €11.8bn, an incremental margin of about 32%. Of that increase, roughly €0.5bn to €0.7bn is genuine fixed-cost absorption (overheads, research and development and factory fixed costs growing more slowly than revenue). The rest is volume on the backlog at today's margins, price and mix already contracted in the backlog, and self-help. This makes FY2027 to FY2028 profit unusually insensitive to small revenue misses and highly sensitive to segment margin outcomes. Against the last four to six years the pattern is the reverse of history: from FY2019 to FY2025 profit was driven by large margin events (wind charges, then their reversal) while revenue grew only 5.2% a year; from FY2026 to FY2030 profit is driven by steady conversion of a priced backlog on revenue growing about 11% a year.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • The history is not a guide to leverage. From FY2019 to FY2025 revenue rose from €28.8bn to €39.1bn (5.2% a year; 7.3% from FY2020). FactSet's adjusted EBIT margin went from 1.9% to -10.6% (FY2023) to 5.1%. Between FY2023 and FY2025 revenue rose 26% and the margin before special items moved 15 points (from -8.9% to 6.0%). Almost all of that swing was wind charges reversing and pricing, not volume. The business has never shown a clean volume-leverage cycle under its current structure.
  • The fixed-cost base. The visible fixed and semi-fixed costs are depreciation and amortisation (about €1.5bn excluding acquired intangibles; €1,781m in total in FY2025), selling, general and administrative costs (€3,492m in FY2025, 8.9% of revenue, down from 9.8% in FY2024) and research and development (€1,210m, flat on FY2024, 3.1% of revenue). With factory overheads and engineering, fixed and semi-fixed costs are probably 25% to 30% of revenue. The company does not disclose plant utilisation or a fixed-variable split, so this is an estimate.
  • Why the incremental margin is about 30% to 34%, not the 42% seen in Q3 FY2026 excluding wind. Capacity is being added at the same time as volume: large gas turbine output goes from 35 to 50 units in FY2027, medium units from 50 to 80 and towards 100 by FY2028, transformer and switchgear capacity rises 50% by 2030, and net headcount has risen by 7,000 in two years. New capacity brings new depreciation (my depreciation rises from €1.5bn to €1.8bn by FY2028) and ramp-up inefficiency (NKT's Karlskrona expansion ran €300m, or 30%, over budget and is being ramped line by line). So part of the volume is absorbed by new fixed cost. My incremental margins: 34% (FY2027), 31% (FY2028), 28% (FY2029), 29% (FY2030).
  • Decomposition of the FY2026 to FY2028 EBITA increase of €3.82bn (my estimates).
  • Volume on the backlog at FY2026 segment margins: about €1.9bn (half). Gas Services €0.83bn, Grid €0.98bn, Transformation of Industry €0.09bn. This is backlog-locked: the €162bn backlog covers about 87% of FY2027 revenue.
  • Margin uplift in Gas Services, Grid and Transformation of Industry: about €1.28bn (one-third). I attribute about €0.6bn to €0.8bn of it to price and mix already in the backlog (new gas orders about 20% above backlog pricing; grid backlog margin rising about 300bps a year) and about €0.5bn to €0.7bn to fixed-cost absorption (selling, general and administrative costs falling from 8.9% to about 8.0% of revenue, and research and development from about 2.9% to about 2.4%).
  • Self-help: about €0.6bn (one-sixth). Siemens Gamesa from €14m to €371m, and the brand royalty saving of €250m.
  • Sensitivity. In FY2027, 1% of revenue (about €0.5bn) at a 35% drop-through is about €0.17bn of EBITA, or about 2.4% of EPS. 100bps of group margin is about €0.49bn, or about 6.8% of EPS; I agree with the first set that Deutsche Bank's "9.6% of EPS per 50bps" is overstated. Siemens Gamesa remains the most leveraged unit: each 1pp of its margin is about €0.1bn.
  • What happens in a downturn, and when it could bite. Fixed-cost leverage works in reverse with a decremental margin of about 35% to 40%. The precedents are severe: Siemens Power and Gas fell from a 16% margin in 2014 to about 5% in 2018, and GE Power's margin halved to about 7% in 2017, because overcapacity triggered price cuts on top of volume loss. For FY2027 to FY2028 this is not a live risk, because volume is contracted. From FY2029 it is: a 10% revenue shortfall against my FY2029 base would cost about €2.1bn to €2.5bn of EBITA (about 20%), before any price effect.
  • Peers. GE Vernova's EBITDA is expected by Bernstein to double from 2027 to 2030; Hitachi Energy targets 15% (FY2028) and Bernstein models 16% by 2030; ABB raised its operational margin from 11% (2019) to about 21% (2026) mainly through mix and pricing, not only volume. Siemens Energy's leverage profile is closer to GE Vernova's (equipment plus a service annuity) than to ABB's.

What it means for the line items.

Line FY2019 to FY2025 FY2026 to FY2030 (my base)
Revenue growth a year 5.2% (7.3% from FY2020) about 11% (FY2025 to FY2030 compound 10.9%)
Margin before special items from about 2% to 6%, via -8.9% in FY2023 from 6.0% to 18.4%
Incremental EBITA margin not meaningful (driven by charges) 28% to 34%
Depreciation (excluding acquired intangibles) about €1.2bn to €1.4bn €1.5bn rising to €2.1bn (3.2% to 3.5% of revenue)
Selling, general and administrative costs 9.7% average FY2022 to FY2024 falling to about 8.0% by FY2028 (target 8.7%)
5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7680P-0270
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?B+802nd of 19full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

A701st of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: There is no financial gearing and there will be none in the forecast period, so financial leverage shapes EPS only through buybacks and interest income, and both effects are modest: buybacks add about 1% to 1.5% a year to EPS after the interest forgone, and net financial items swing from a €293m cost in FY2025 to a small income of €20m to €100m a year. The balance sheet's real leverage is to customers, not lenders: about €8bn of net cash sits against €22.3bn of contract liabilities, so the group is in effect financed by its customers. As those prepayments normalise from FY2028, and with about €4.5bn spent on Siemens Energy India, net cash falls to about €4.5bn in FY2028 and stays around €4.5bn to €5.5bn to FY2030, while the €3bn a year buyback I assume remains affordable but leaves little room for a downside surprise in cash.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • Starting point (Q3 FY2026). Cash €11.1bn, debt €3.0bn on FactSet's definition (which includes leases of about €2.1bn), net cash €8.1bn, net cash about 1.5 times EBITDA. Moody's Baa1 stable. An undrawn €4bn revolving credit facility and a €9bn bank guarantee facility, which replaced the state guarantees in 2025.
  • Net financial items. FY2025 net interest was -€293m, including guarantee fees that ended with the exit from the state guarantees (more than €100m a year, management). In the first nine months of FY2026 interest income was €227m and interest expense €141m. With gross cash of about €10bn to €11bn earning about 3% in FY2027, and bond, loan, lease and pension interest of about €210m, net financial items are about +€100m in FY2027, falling to about +€20m to +€40m in FY2028 to FY2030 as cash is spent on India and buybacks. The swing of about +€0.35bn from FY2025 to FY2026 is worth about €0.30 of EPS: the largest single non-operating EPS gain in the forecast.
  • Buybacks. €3.05bn in FY2026 (tranches one and two, plus the start of tranche three on 24 September 2026). Tranche three runs to 31 March 2027 for up to €2bn. I assume the pace rises to about €3bn a year from FY2027, as Morgan Stanley expects to be announced on 11 November, against a baseline of about €2bn a year. That retires about 2.5% of the shares a year at buyback prices rising from about €130 (FY2027) to about €180 (FY2030), taking the average share count from about 845m (FY2026) to 768m (FY2030), about 10% below the FY2025 year-end 855m.
  • What buybacks are really worth. If the €9bn bought back over FY2026 to FY2028 had stayed in cash at about 3% before tax, FY2028 EPS would be about €8.07 instead of €8.36. So buybacks net of the interest forgone add about 3.6% to FY2028 EPS and about 6% to FY2030 EPS, roughly 1% to 1.5% a year. The buyback is not price-dynamic: an independent bank executes within a 25% of average daily volume limit, and the second tranche was bought at an average of €154.63, well above today's €118.68. A lower share price does raise accretion (€3bn buys about 25m shares at €120 against 20m at €150), but the company cannot accelerate sharply into weakness.
  • Balance sheet path (my base). Net cash about €8.1bn at FY2026, €8.9bn at FY2027, about €4.5bn at FY2028 after the €4.5bn India payment, then about €4.6bn and €5.5bn. Total shareholder returns over FY2026 to FY2028 are about €13.3bn (buybacks €9.05bn and dividends €4.3bn), above the "up to €10bn" framed at the November 2025 Capital Markets Day, because I assume the buyback step-up.
  • The leverage that matters: customer prepayments. Contract liabilities of €22.3bn fund inventories and contract assets. If prepayments simply stop growing, cash conversion falls to about one times net income. If they reverse, cash can fall fast: Morgan Stanley's bear case of a full €16.2bn reversal would, over several years, turn net cash into net debt of about €8bn to €10bn. That is still less than one times EBITDA, so it is not a solvency risk, but it would stop buybacks and is the mechanism behind the GE Power precedent of 2017 to 2019. Netting contract liabilities, the group's "prepayment-adjusted" position is about -€14bn in FY2026 and about -€13bn to -€14bn in FY2030: the balance sheet does not de-lever in substance even as headline net cash stays positive.
  • India and Transformation of Industry. The India stake costs about €4.5bn (Deutsche Bank; Jefferies warns it could exceed €4.4bn as the two Indian share prices move). A Transformation of Industry sale above €10bn (enterprise value, reported bids; Jefferies €13bn, Deutsche Bank €12bn) would more than refill the cash, and ABB returned all of its $7.6bn to $7.8bn Power Grids proceeds to shareholders. Neither proceeds nor their return are in my base case.

For EPS and the balance sheet: EPS gains about €0.30 from financial items in FY2026 and about €0.50 from the falling share count by FY2028; the balance sheet stays net cash throughout, but the headroom shrinks from about €8bn to about €4.5bn while the prepayment cushion unwinds. A sharper prepayment reversal is the one path on which financial leverage becomes a constraint, and it would bind on buybacks first.

Cost-out scope NTM

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

Judgement: A meaningful but secondary pool of reducible cost, roughly €1bn of annual run-rate potential over FY2026 to FY2028. It is small against the pricing-driven margin uplift, but nearly all upside to consensus if executed.

Cost item Reducible amount Timing
Siemens brand royalty €51m to €400m a year, removed through Omterra From late 2026
Wind non-conformance and quality costs Part of €450m a year of quality cash outflows; integration savings target €300m a year; onshore factories 10 to 4; fewer variants Through FY2028

Not cuttable without harm:

  • Direct materials (40% to 50% of cost of goods in grid; passed through).
  • Skilled labour (scarce).

Reasoning: Bruch's Linde record was +700bps of margin through disciplined bidding and productivity. Siemens Energy's priority is productivity (robotics and AI on the shop floor, squeezing output from existing sites) rather than headcount cuts. Net headcount is +7,000.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

A631st of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Cost elimination is a real but secondary contributor: about €0.25bn of EBITA in FY2027 and about €0.6bn to €0.8bn by FY2028 against FY2026, roughly one-sixth of the FY2026 to FY2028 EBITA increase. Two items dominate: the end of the Siemens brand royalty (my base €100m in FY2027 and about €250m a year from FY2028) and the fall in selling, general and administrative costs as a share of revenue (from 8.9% to about 8.0% by FY2028, which beats the 8.7% target). Wind cost-out is mostly delivered already and now acts through absorption and quality, not new cuts. Against these sit separation and rebrand costs (€0.3bn to €0.5bn a year in FY2027 to FY2028, in special items, so outside adjusted EPS but inside cash) and stranded costs of about €0.1bn a year once Transformation of Industry leaves. Cost is not the swing factor in this model; price, conversion and wind quality are.

Reasoning and critical evidence, item by item:

Item Size When it reaches the accounts Confidence
Brand royalty to Siemens AG (ends with the Omterra rebrand, which starts late 2026 and is phased over about 18 months) My base: €100m in FY2027, €250m in FY2028, rising with revenue to about €290m by FY2030 (0.45pp of margin). Evidence spans €51m to €400m From H2 FY2027; full from FY2028 Medium on timing; low on size
Selling, general and administrative leverage Cost growing about 7% a year against revenue at 12% to 13%: about €0.4bn to €0.5bn by FY2028 against a constant 8.9% ratio Gradual, every year Medium-high
Research and development leverage Spending grows about 4% a year from €1.21bn: about €0.25bn by FY2028 against a constant ratio Gradual Medium (it is already low at 3.1%, below ABB and Siemens AG)
Guarantee fees More than €100m a year Already gone; sits in net financial items, not EBITA High
Siemens Gamesa integration savings (€300m), onshore factories from 10 to 4, fewer variants Mostly delivered by FY2026 Now visible as absorption on flat revenue High that it is done; medium that it holds
Wind quality cost (about €450m a year of quality cash, "mid-triple-digit millions" guided for FY2026) Falls as the 4.X and 5.X remediation completes Cash, through provision use; P&L only if provisions are released or topped up Low; this is the tail risk
Separation and rebrand one-off costs €0.3bn to €0.5bn a year in FY2027 and FY2028 Special items: hit reported EPS and cash, not adjusted EPS Medium
Stranded corporate costs after Transformation of Industry leaves About €0.1bn a year (ABB: about 40bps and $125m pro forma after its Robotics sale) Only when deconsolidated, FY2028 at the earliest Medium
  • The royalty, reasoned. The trademark fee is margin-linked: 0.3% of relevant revenue, rising to 1.2% once the margin passes 9% (Jefferies, July 2026; Deutsche Bank describes the same laddered structure). The FY2024 annual-report figure of €51m was set at a 1% margin and so at the floor; it cannot describe FY2026 at 12%. Applying 1.2% to the full group revenue would give about €0.5bn, which exceeds Jefferies' €400m, so the relevant revenue base is probably narrower than the group. Contract exit terms, any termination payment and the exact end date are not disclosed. I therefore take about €250m from FY2028, with €400m as the upside case and about €100m as the downside case. In my base the saving is worth about 0.45pp of FY2028 margin and about €0.23 of FY2028 EPS.
  • Selling, general and administrative costs, reasoned. The ratio already fell from 9.83% (FY2024) to 8.94% (FY2025). A target of 8.7% for FY2028 is barely below the FY2025 level and looks set before the revenue acceleration. Even with 7,000 more staff, wage inflation and separation work, cost growth of about 7% against revenue growth of 12% to 13% takes the ratio to about 8.0%. GE Vernova targets $600m of general and administrative savings by 2028; ABB cut its ratio from 19.1% to 17.8% in a year. The risk is that the saving is reinvested in commercial and engineering staff for the ramp.
  • What does not belong in the cost story. Direct materials (40% to 45% of transformer cost) are passed through by indexation; skilled labour is the binding constraint and cannot be cut; and the management's chosen route is productivity on existing sites, not headcount reduction (Spanish wind job cuts were negotiated down from 423 to 255 under co-determination).
  • Timing summary (EBITA, against FY2026): FY2027 about +€0.25bn (royalty +€0.1bn, overhead leverage +€0.15bn); FY2028 about +€0.65bn (royalty +€0.25bn, overhead and research leverage about +€0.4bn); FY2029 to FY2030 about +€0.8bn to +€0.9bn, and about €0.1bn less if Transformation of Industry is deconsolidated with stranded costs.
5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4780P-0558
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?B+802nd of 19full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

B584th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: The loops act on different lines at different times, and the timing mismatch is the key fact for the model. Reinforcing loops (backlog repricing, installed-base service growth, a fragile wind recovery) dominate revenue and margins through FY2028 and keep margins rising, more slowly, to FY2030. The main balancing loop, the prepayment loop, has already peaked in cash terms: it turns from a large inflow in FY2026 (about €3.6bn of working capital) to a small inflow in FY2027 and to outflows of about €0.8bn to €1.5bn a year in FY2028 to FY2030. So cash peaks in FY2026, while profit keeps rising to FY2030; free cash flow falls from 1.7 times adjusted net income in FY2026 to about 0.8 times in FY2028 to FY2030. The competitor-capacity and customer-goodwill loops are balancing loops that act on new-order prices from about 2028 and on reported margins mostly after FY2030. The affordability loop in US utilities acts on grid orders in FY2027 to FY2028 and on grid revenue growth from FY2029.

The loops, ranked by effect on the model (FY2027 to FY2029):

Loop Type now Mechanism, step by step Effect on the lines When it turns
1. Prepayment and working capital Reinforcing for cash today; balancing from FY2028 Orders above revenue raise contract liabilities (€22.3bn) and cash, which funds buybacks; when book-to-bill nears 1 inflows stop, and as prepaid units ship, contract liabilities fall Working capital: +€3.6bn (FY2026), +€1.0bn (FY2027), -€0.8bn (FY2028), -€1.5bn (FY2029), -€1.2bn (FY2030) Inflow fades in FY2027; outflow from FY2028 as FY2025 to FY2026 gas prepayments convert into deliveries
2. Backlog repricing Reinforcing Old low-price contracts ship out; new ones carry about 20% higher gas prices and about 300bps a year higher grid backlog margins Gas Services margin 16.5% to 20.0% (FY2026 to FY2028); Grid 17.4% to 20.0% Fades from FY2029 as the repricing step is used up; reverses only on orders booked after peer capacity lands
3. Installed base and services Reinforcing, slow Each turbine shipped adds a long-term agreement (over 70% attach rate, 17 years); service revenue starts three to four years after the unit leaves the factory, modifications after six to seven Service growth high single to low double digits throughout; Deutsche Bank: 160 GW of additions could add €3.6bn of annual service revenue within the decade Strengthens after FY2029 as the FY2026 to FY2028 deliveries enter service; this is the margin floor in FY2030 and beyond
4. Wind Turning from balancing to reinforcing, fragile Quality failures led to share loss, under-absorption and losses; selectivity and fewer platforms now lift margin on flat volume; credible profits would rebuild orders Siemens Gamesa margin 0.1% (FY2026) to 3.5% (FY2028), 5.0% (FY2030), on flat revenue Can reverse at once on a serial quality issue (the Taiwan offshore fire of August 2026 was the reminder)
5. Competitor capacity Balancing, delayed Scarcity pricing attracts capacity: GE Vernova to 30 GW by 2030; Mitsubishi +30% by FY2028 and $490m at Savannah; Hitachi's $528m Mississippi plant (2029); Korean makers at 19% of US transformer imports No effect on FY2027 to FY2028 reported margins; caps new-order pricing from 2028 to 2029; Jefferies sees gas equipment prices falling towards $1,800 per kW by 2035 New-order prices from about 2028; reported margins from about FY2030
6. US affordability (data-centre loop) Balancing, early Grid queues push behind-the-meter gas; rising bills bring the Ratepayer Protection Act (House 417 to 3) and capex trims (PG&E -$2bn for 2027) Slows US grid order growth in FY2027 to FY2028; I model Grid revenue growth easing from +20% (FY2027) to +9% (FY2030) Orders now; revenue from FY2029
7. Customer goodwill Balancing, latent Customers describe Siemens Energy as "transactional"; utilities are diversifying away from single sources Little effect while slots are scarce; share and price give-back once lead times normalise 2029 and beyond
8. Reflexive buyback Weakly reinforcing A lower share price retires more shares for the same euros About +1pp of EPS for each 20% fall in the buyback price; the mandate limits acceleration Continuous; weak

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • Why the prepayment loop turns earlier than consensus assumes. Group book-to-bill was 1.7 in the first nine months of FY2026 (orders €53.3bn, revenue €31.4bn) and Q4 orders are expected to slow to about €16.5bn (Deutsche Bank). Gas Services Q3 orders rose 61% and are "not expected to be repeated every quarter". As gas orders normalise in FY2027 to FY2028, group book-to-bill falls towards 1.1 to 1.2 in FY2027 and close to 1.0 in FY2029. Because the large FY2025 to FY2026 gas prepayments convert into deliveries in FY2028 to FY2030, contract liabilities can fall even at a book-to-bill near 1. Bernstein's model (working-capital days from -78 to -30 by FY2028, about €3.4bn of outflow) is the closest published estimate; I spread a similar amount over FY2028 to FY2030. Management says reservation agreements convert to firm orders within six months, carry advance payments and that it targets cash conversion above 1 through the cycle; the first point supports the quality of orders, but none of them stops the arithmetic of prepaid units being delivered.
  • The precedent. At GE Power in 2017 to 2019, book-to-bill fell below 1, progress collections were worked off without replacement, and billions of working capital flowed out while reported profit fell more slowly. Vestas shows the same pattern in smaller form (service contract assets rose from €0.9bn to €1.2bn in 2025). My base is a normalisation, not a GE Power-style collapse; the GE Power path is the bear case (P-10).
  • Interplay. Loops 2 and 3 make profit forecastable; loop 1 makes cash less forecastable. Loops 5 to 7 all hit at the same time (2028 to 2030) and on the same variable (new-order price), so they are correlated, not diversifying. That is why I lean below consensus in FY2029 to FY2030 and not before.
  • Peers. GE Vernova runs the same prepayment and slot model (15% to 20% non-refundable down payments; 43 GW of its 83 GW gas backlog in slot reservations priced 10% to 20% above the existing backlog), and is the competitor most likely to change pricing behaviour when its 30 GW is in place. Mitsubishi is the most conservative capacity adder. Prysmian funds high-voltage cable working capital with hyperscaler advances, the same loop in cables.

Effect on the main lines. Revenue and EBITA: positive and reinforcing to FY2028, still positive but slowing in FY2029 to FY2030. Free cash flow: falls from €6.9bn (FY2026) to €5.2bn (FY2028) even as EBITA rises 71%. Net cash: plateaus at about €4.5bn to €5.5bn after FY2028. The multiple: the balancing loops that the market prices now (peak orders, capacity) do not appear in reported profit until about FY2030, which keeps the earnings-versus-share-price divergence of the last six months alive.

Guidance is conservative, reasonably specific, and has been raised on evidence each year since FY2024. That is a supportive pattern for forecast credibility. But consensus has already moved above the formal FY2028 targets, so the guidance "cushion" is now mostly priced into estimates. The next reference point is the FY2030 framework on 11 November 2026.

The Consumer should:

  1. Build FY2027 to FY2028 from the backlog and segment run-rates, not from the guide.
  2. Expect the initial FY2027 guide to be below a backlog-based model.
  3. Treat cash guidance as the least informative part: inputs are not disclosed, and its dependence on prepayments is cyclical.

FY2028:

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

A781st of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Very conservative. The FY2028 CMD targets (group 14% to 16%; Gas and Grid 18% to 20%; Siemens Gamesa 3% to 5%; low-teens revenue growth) have already been nearly reached at segment level in FY2026, two years early, while the input forces strengthened after the targets were set:

  • Gas market from 100 GW to 110 to 120 GW.
  • Pricing +20%.
  • Commitments 95 GW.
  • A 10% transformer deficit to 2030.

Reasoning:

  • Against history, the targets look bold: the margin rose from 1% to 6% over FY2024 to FY2025, and the best margin before special items in the decade was about 6%.
  • Against today's inputs they are timid:
  • Q3 FY2026 Grid already at 19.9%.
  • Gas Services at 17.3% in a seasonally weak quarter.
  • The group at 14.2% (flattered by one-offs).
  • The adjustment for input forces runs the other way for FY2029-and-beyond. The input force (order growth) is near its peak, so FY2030 targets set now could prove less conservative than the FY2028 ones were.
  • The Consumer should treat the FY2027 to FY2028 guide as conservative by about 1 to 2 points of margin, and the FY2030 target, when set, as closer to fair.

The places consensus is most likely wrong:

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

B554th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Five things are uncommon:

  1. The KPI is "profit before special items" (EBITA-like), not EBIT. Special items have been large (restructuring, remediation, disposals). The Consumer must keep the company's KPI, FactSet's adjusted EBIT and the brokers' EBIT apart.
  2. Pre-tax free cash flow guidance. This excludes tax. FactSet's post-tax free cash flow (operating cash flow less capex) differs.
  3. Strategy resets happen at full-year results. The 11 November FY2026 results will carry FY2030 targets, a capital allocation update and FY2027 guidance at once. That makes it the year's single binary information event.
  4. The investor relations team is active on roadshows (Paris; New York; Q4 pre-close call), and the pre-close calls are used to steer consensus. For example, the September 2026 pre-close call confirmed €3.6bn of returns and the Transformation of Industry timing.
  5. Disclosure gaps on prepayments and pricing, and analysts' consensus sources differ: Q3 consensus figures varied by source, for example Gas Services orders €8,287m against €8,475m.

Reasoning: For the model, the main practical issue is definitional. Build segments on profit before special items, add explicit special items, and reconcile to the brokers' EPS basis (FY2025 €1.63, equal to reported basic).
