Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

D529th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Five patterns are relevant. Siemens Energy fits the late-stage version of the long-cycle upturn: earnings rising strongly while the market has started to price the order peak.

  1. Long-cycle capital goods shares peak with order growth, not with earnings. Earnings keep rising for two to three years after the order peak as backlog converts. The market starts to de-rate when the order second derivative turns, often 12 to 24 months before the earnings peak. Siemens Energy fits exactly: the share price is -32% over six months while FY2027 EPS is +11%. The sector digest: "the first half of a one-to-two-year holding in the paused conversion cohorts is the cleaner half."
  2. The prepayment and service-asset false friend. GE Power (2017 to 2018; outside the files) and Vestas (contract assets of about €1.2bn, a €312m write-down in 2024) show that strong reported earnings in long-cycle equipment can coexist with deteriorating cash quality. When orders fall, progress collections reverse and free cash flow collapses before reported profit does. Siemens Energy's contract liabilities (€22.3bn) make this the main tail mechanism. Our risk file's competing-hypotheses work selects the "false friend" profile.
  3. Pricing-power cycles end through capacity, with a lag. The Wisdom Library entries "bottleneck migration" and "the early-mover window is not a moat" apply. The current bottleneck owners (heavy equipment) capture early rents. The constraint migrates next to skilled labour and to grid connections themselves.
  4. Wind turnarounds take longer than planned. Vestas took 2022 to 2026 to go from a -11% to an 8% to 9% margin. Siemens Gamesa's breakeven arrived abruptly in Q3, a binary inflection that our change-forces file flags as analytically opaque. Rule of thumb: "worser, odder, longer".
  5. Sandbagging plus a sector-wide beat-and-raise is partly a sector story. Seven of eleven energy contractors beat and raised in the same months. Siemens Energy's beats are partly the cycle, not only management.

Outperformance came from:

  • Owning the scarce asset.
  • Converting backlog at better prices.
  • Self-help repair (Vestas warranty from 6.4% to 3% of revenue; ABB's 11% to 20% margin under the "ABB Way").

Underperformance came from:

  • Quality failures (Siemens Gamesa 2023, Western Link at Prysmian in 2018: -38% share price).
  • Capacity overshoot.
  • Cash reversals.

Where Siemens Energy fits: It owns scarce assets, it is in conversion, and it is still repairing. Positive for earnings delivery; negative for the timing of the multiple.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

B+453rd of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: About 20% to 25% on unchanged estimates, and about 35% if estimates are also cut, implying a trough zone of roughly €78 to €95 against €118.68 today.

Workings:

Case 24-month-forward EPS P/E Price Change
Ten-year "crash" band, unchanged EPS €8.22 11.2x €92 -22%
Five-year 10th percentile, unchanged EPS €8.22 11.23x about €92 about -22%
Crash band with a 15% EPS cut (gas order collapse plus a wind charge) about €7.0 11.2x about €78 -34%
Market prices FY2028 as peak earnings FY2028 consensus €8.35 11x about €92 about -22%
  • Today: 14.3x on 24-month-forward EPS of €8.22. That is the 29th percentile of five years and the lowest of the last two years.
  • Cross-checks:
  • Bear targets: Jefferies €116 (in a 25% discount sum-of-the-parts), Morgan Stanley €125, Barclays €130, mwb research €100.
  • The 52-week low is about €100 (12.2x current 24-month EPS).
  • Peers at trough-like multiples: Nexans 10.5x, Prysmian 13.9x P/E.

Reasoning:

  • A fair trough multiple for a business with this net cash, service annuity and visible FY2027 to FY2028 earnings is about 11x to 12x forward earnings.
  • Much of the de-rating has already happened. The five-year peak was 28.2x, and the two-year median is 22.5x.
  • Downside to fair trough is now smaller than at any time in the last two years.
  • But the multiple path is governed by the order cycle and the AI-financing regime, not by FY2027 earnings delivery.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

B+703rd of 19

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative has shifted from "turnaround" (2024 to 2025) to "AI-power compounder" (to mid-2026) to "peak orders, de-rating" (the last six months). It did so while the fundamentals kept improving.

The staircase ahead is rich in dated, well-founded delivery steps. The FY2030 targets, buybacks and the Transformation of Industry decision are the novel steps that can restart a positive narrative. But a narrative restart needs either evidence that orders do not collapse in 2027 or a portfolio event that is new to the market (a Transformation of Industry sale price, or a wind exit).

For later sets, the narrative question is not whether earnings rise to FY2028; the staircase says they do. The question is whether the market will capitalise those earnings as peak.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–74

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: Highly congruent on structure. The two sets support each other on every load-bearing judgement: revenue to FY2028 is set by backlog conversion and capacity rather than demand; margins rise mechanically as repriced orders ship; cash peaks before profit; the cyclical exposure sits in FY2029 and beyond; formal guidance is a floor; the multiple is the least predictable variable. The tensions are of degree and timing, not direction, and each resolves cleanly. The new reports change four things: they enlarge the prepayment pool (strengthening the cash thesis in both directions), they settle the brand-fee size and leave only its timing open, they move the wind tail from onshore to offshore, and they expose a basis mismatch between the second set's working-capital inputs and the Financial Analyst's model that has put €21bn of spurious cash into the model's FY2026 without touching earnings per share. None of these changes the shape of the 18-month forecast. Together they make profit slightly more predictable and cash slightly less.

Where the sets align, and what the new evidence adds (the load-bearing agreements):

  1. Revenue is capacity- and price-limited, not demand-limited (first set C-01, C-06, C-80; second set P-01, P-10). Corroborated: the large gas turbine ramp from 35 to 50 units in FY2027 was "fully on track" at the 30 September pre-close; medium turbines reached about 80 units in FY2026 (from 50); Gas Services backlog €73bn and Grid €51bn at Q3 FY2026; the FY2025 annual report planned about €11bn of Gas Services backlog conversion in FY2026 and a further €9bn in FY2027.
  2. Margins rise mechanically through FY2028 (C-17, C-47; P-04, P-05). Corroborated by the 30 September pre-close ("new orders carry substantially higher margins than the executed backlog and than orders signed in early FY2026") and by GE Vernova's Q3 2026 on 28 October (gas pricing up 50% year on year; Power EBITDA margin 21.4%; slots booked into 2032). Morgan Stanley's 28 October note adds the one cautionary nuance: GE Vernova's gas backlog margin rose 1,100bps in 2025 against Siemens Energy's 500bps, because of its higher US share. Siemens Energy's repricing is real but slower than the bellwether's.
  3. Cash peaks in FY2026 while profit rises to FY2030 (first set C-52 as modified by the second set; P-05, P-08). Reinforced: the €5.4bn nine-month contract-liability inflow explains the FY2026 peak and sets the size of the later unwind.
  4. The cyclical risk is dated FY2029 and beyond (C-02, C-28, C-31; P-04, P-10). Corroborated by Morgan Stanley (26 October: pricing stabilises within 12 to 24 months and price-cost turns negative on orders delivered beyond 2030), Ambit (flat Indian transformer pricing against copper +40%; tier-two entrants from FY2028), and Deutsche Bank (OEM gas turbine capacity rising to 78 to 100 GW by 2030 against a 47 GW baseline).
  5. Guidance is a floor and consensus sits above the formal FY2028 range (C-68, C-71; P-06). Corroborated: Deutsche Bank expects the 14% to 16% FY2028 margin to be pulled forward to FY2027 on 11 November; Morgan Stanley expects a 20% to 22% FY2030 target against a 19.8% consensus; buy-side expectations run above the sell-side's (Morgan Stanley, 2 October).
  6. The multiple, not earnings, is where the unpredictability lives (C-07, C-82, C-83). The co-movement table quantifies it (D-07): 62% of the stock's daily variance is now explained by the peer basket, with a beta of 1.5.

Where the sets jar, and my resolution of each:

  • (a) Does consensus free cash flow already assume neutral working capital? C-52 said yes; P-05 and P-08 said no. The second set is right, and the case is now stronger. Consensus FY2027 and FY2028 free cash flow of €6.8bn a year (FactSet, before leases) with consensus capital spending falling to €1.9bn implies near-neutral working capital and light investment at the same time. The Q3 balance sheet shows the prepayment pool still growing fast (+€5.75bn in nine months). So consensus is about right for FY2027, when the inflow is still running, and wrong for FY2028 to FY2029, when it reverses. This matters for I-02: the free cash flow line is the one most likely to be cut.
  • (b) Share-count attrition. The first set said about 3% a year, the second 2.5% (buyback prices rising with earnings). The second set's arithmetic holds; the difference is about 1% of FY2028 EPS.
  • (c) The brand fee. The first set left €51m to €400m open; the second set took €100m in FY2027 and €250m from FY2028 as a middle value. The new evidence settles the size at about €400m at today's margin and leaves the stop date open. The second set's €250m for FY2028 is therefore not a central estimate of a continuous range; it is a fair expected value of a two-point distribution: about €400m if payments stop as entities rebrand through FY2027 to FY2028, about zero before FY2030 if they run to the contract's end. I keep the input and treat it as bimodal in D-04. It is also partly priced: Rothschild & Co Redburn and Deutsche Bank include the saving; the narrative report finds most other brokers "projecting flat margin-linked fees until 2030".
  • (d) Grid price-cost. C-05 feared a squeeze on FY2028 to FY2029 deliveries because pass-through formulas are undisclosed; the second set built a deliberately slow grid margin path (19.3% in FY2027, 20.0% in FY2028, then +0.5pp a year) to allow for it. The own-5 report shows the protection is better than the first set assumed: back-to-back escalation clauses linked to metal indices (Deutsche Bank, 21 May 2026), more than 95% local sourcing, derivatives on copper and aluminium, reservation agreements converting to firm orders within six to twelve months (so pricing is set close to the manufacturing start), and the lesson of 2021, when older contracts without adjustment clauses forced the group to absorb inflation. Peers show the same: Hitachi Energy has 70% of recent orders on flexible pricing; HD Hyundai Electric passed 84% of tariff costs through in 2025 and earned a 24.1% margin in Q3 2026. The residual exposures are short-cycle products without escalation clauses, a three- to six-month indexation lag, and India (flat pricing against copper +40%, where Siemens Energy India is consolidated only from FY2028). Resolution: the squeeze risk inside the 18 months is lower than the first set implied, the second set's grid path is adequately cautious, and the residual skew on the FY2027 grid margin is up (the Q3 FY2026 print was 19.9% and the nine-month run-rate about 17.6%).
  • (e) Wind. C-81 ranked a second onshore warranty wave as the main sudden risk. The two Taiwan incidents on SG 14 turbines within two months re-point that risk at the offshore platform that holds a pipeline above 22 GW and about two-thirds of the Western offshore market. The magnitude of a systemic finding is unchanged (€1bn to €2bn), the probability is modestly higher, and the time to resolution is short: root-cause findings should come within one or two quarters. The second set's 1.5% FY2027 wind margin stands as a base; the cone around it is wider on the downside.
  • (f) The model and the inputs: the working-capital basis. The second set's "Working capital (% of revenue)" inputs (-20.9% for FY2026 to -10.1% for FY2030) are on the company's basis, with contract liabilities netted, and the second set warned the Financial Analyst to replace the model's FactSet-basis FY2025 opening balance (+€16,064m, 41.1% of revenue: receivables plus inventories less payables) with about -14.1%. The model kept the FactSet opening. The FY2026 working-capital flow it computed is therefore +€16,064m minus (-€9,147m), about +€25.2bn, instead of the intended +€3.6bn: a €21.6bn overstatement, which is the flagged gap (€28,193m against €6,870m, a difference of €21.3bn; the small residual is cash tax and special-item cash). EPS is untouched; the model's net cash is overstated by about €21bn to €26bn in every year (its €34.2bn at FY2026 against the second set's €8.1bn). From FY2027 the model's working-capital flows are right because the inputs set year-on-year changes. The fix is in inputs-changed.md: the line restated on the model's own basis so that the changes equal the second set's intended flows. The other, unflagged gaps are explained and need no change: FY2026 EPS €4.57 against €4.71 arises because the model's FY2026 is nine months of reported actuals (after special items, at a higher effective tax rate) plus one forecast quarter; FY2029 and FY2030 EPS are €0.16 and €0.30 above the second set because minorities (€154m and €174m, about €0.20 and €0.23 a share) are not modelled, exactly as the second set warned; FY2027 to FY2030 free cash flow is €44m to €287m below the second set because the model charges cash tax at the P&L rate rather than the guided sub-20% cash rate, partly offset elsewhere. One more mechanical point: the model lifted the second set's Q4 FY2026 EBITDA margin input (14.6%, brokers' basis) by 2.6 points to reconcile it with its own quarterly history, which is FactSet operating EBITDA after special items. The annual figures are unaffected, but the model's Q4 FY2026 margin of about 17% must not be compared with the 11 November print; the second set's expectation for Q4 is profit before special items of about €1.41bn, an 11.4% margin, on revenue of about €12.35bn.
  • (g) FY2026 cash. The second set's +€3.6bn working-capital inflow for FY2026 against nine months of +€5.4bn from contract liabilities (less €2.8bn absorbed by contract assets and inventories) implies a flat fourth quarter. Deutsche Bank calls the €8bn pre-tax guide conservative; nine-month pre-tax free cash flow was €7.16bn, so the guide implies only €0.84bn in Q4. Upside of €0.5bn to €1bn to the FY2026 cash print is likely. It is immaterial for the 18-month forecasts and material for the tone of 11 November.
  • (h) Transformation of Industry. Both sets keep it consolidated and the own-4 report confirms the timing (no decision before mid-2027; closing late 2027 or FY2028; IFRS 5 reclassification to discontinued operations probably in H2 FY2027; a 60% sale with 40% retained, per Jefferies; €10bn to €13bn of value). It adds one fact the sets did not have: the supervisory board and the works council rejected the June 2026 spin-off proposal, which forced the pivot to a private sale (Ambit, 4 September). Standalone costs were already €59m in H1 FY2026 against €1m a year earlier. The second set's €0.5bn of FY2027 special-item cash is consistent with that. Two consequences for predictability: FY2027 reported figures will be restated into continuing and discontinued operations in the middle of the forecast window, which breaks consensus comparability for a quarter or two; and labour friction is now evidence, not conjecture, for the first set's "execution overload in 2027" risk.

What the congruence means for the memo. The Consumer can treat the two sets as one model with one open mechanical fix. The joints that carry the case (backlog, repricing, cash-before-profit, FY2029 cyclicality, guidance as floor) are triple-sourced across the sets and the new reports. The places where the sets differed are now settled: on cash the second set, on the royalty a bimodal reading, on wind an offshore re-pointing, on grid price-cost a lower near-term risk.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

C+552nd of 8

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: The narrative is comprehensive on the dynamics that will not reach the 18-month earnings (the peak in gas orders, competitor capacity and a 2030s price war, the FY2030 margin ambition, wind's turnaround) and thin on the dynamics that will shape the 18-month cash and the binary inputs (the timing and size of the working-capital unwind against a €28.1bn pool, the royalty's stop date, the offshore SG 14 question after Taiwan, the optics of a mid-year move to discontinued operations, grid price-cost on 2028 deliveries, US affordability politics as a Siemens Energy-specific driver). Investor commentary, which the files hold only through brokers' investor-feedback notes and expert calls (no Twitter or retail sources), is concentrated on two questions: is 2026 the order peak, and will the FY2030 target be 20% or more. Both are "known and priced" to the point of over-discounting: the multiple at 14.3x implies, per Morgan Stanley, a terminal margin of 8.5% beyond 2035 against our 18% in FY2030 and a service annuity that grows for two decades. So the narrative gap is favourable on the multiple (the market discounts a known risk whose earnings effect is three years away) and unfavourable on cash (the market has not discounted a lesser-known risk whose cash effect is 12 to 24 months away). Over 18 months to three years, the narrative is where our view is best understood: the sell-side models the FY2029 to FY2030 pricing plateau, if not yet the erosion.

Dynamic by dynamic (houses credited only where the report cites their own documents; dates are the documents'):

Peak gas orders in FY2027 and what it means Morgan Stanley in depth (21 Aug: two scenarios, orders at 134 GW annualised against a 50 GW history; 26 Oct); Deutsche Bank (2 Sep: a sentiment risk, backlog still growing; 13 Sep: order growth decelerating); Jefferies (11 Sep: "peak order myopia"; 28 Oct on GE Vernova: "blunted by peak order pressure"); Rothschild & Co Redburn (15 Oct 2025: "calling time"; 8 Jan 2026: capital cycle risk) Universal; the dominant debate; the main driver of the six-month de-rating Agreed on the fact, disagree on the pricing of it: it barely touches FY2027 to FY2029 EPS
Working-capital and contract-liability normalisation Bernstein (22 Aug 2025: €3bn to €4bn by FY2028; -205 days of contract liabilities); Morgan Stanley (16 and 26 Oct 2026: a €16.2bn full-reversal stress case to €108); Deutsche Bank (6 Aug: listed as a downside risk; 27 Nov 2023: CFO expected stability to FY2027); Ambit (24 Oct: an FY2028 outflow in its model) Known as a mechanism by every house; the timing "poorly quantified" (the narrative report's own words); not in consensus cash numbers This is our largest gap: a larger pool (€28.1bn, which none of the cited notes uses) and a modelled FY2028 to FY2030 outflow
GE Vernova to 30 GW and a 2030s price war Deutsche Bank (12 Oct: 78 to 100 GW of OEM capacity by 2030), Jefferies (11 Sep, 1 Sep: service cushions a 60% equipment fall), Bernstein (23 Jul), Morgan Stanley (26 Oct: equipment margins to 10% by 2035 in the bear) Well known; moderately priced Agreed; it is our FY2030 margin gap with the market
Behind-the-meter gas: bridge or permanent Jefferies (12 Jul), Morgan Stanley (1 Sep: engines as bridging), expert calls Limited; treated as a demand tailwind without the policy risk Our 2027 question; under-articulated
US utility affordability and capex trims Morgan Stanley utilities (13 Oct: rate-case politics in California, Michigan, New York), Jefferies utilities (21 Sep: Ratepayer Protection Act 417 to 3; 22 Oct: Oregon pause) Emerging in utility research; underweight in Siemens Energy research Under-articulated for FY2027 to FY2028 grid orders
Siemens Gamesa's turnaround and quality Bernstein (15 Jul, 5 Aug), Jefferies (18 May), Deutsche Bank (5 Aug, 12 Oct), Morgan Stanley (26 Oct: 4.X and 5.X restart risk), expert calls Extensively covered for onshore; the two Taiwan SG 14 incidents are in the news flow (Bernstein 24 Aug; EnergyWatch 5 Oct) but not yet in the margin debate Under-articulated on the offshore platform
Brand royalty size and timing Jefferies (14 Jul: €300m to €400m; "could end before 2030"), Deutsche Bank (30 Sep: about 1pp; 20% to 22% including it), Rothschild & Co Redburn (26 Nov 2025: ends FY2030; lifts FY2028 profit) Size well known; timing unconfirmed; most models "projecting flat fees until 2030" Our bimodal reading is not articulated anywhere
Transformation of Industry separation and value Deutsche Bank (26 Aug: +50bps), Jefferies (25 Aug: €13bn; 22 Jun: raises the odds of a wind spin-off), Morgan Stanley (26 Oct: €11.8bn), press (more than €10bn; five sponsors), Ambit (4 Sep: Indian minority friction; the June plan rejected) Well known; the discontinued-operations timing and its effect on comparability are not discussed A mid-FY2027 optics risk no one flags
Siemens Energy India purchase and minorities Ambit alone (4 Sep, 8 Oct, 24 Oct) Known in India, invisible in Europe and the US Minor for group earnings; a governance footnote
Buyback pace at 11 November Morgan Stanley (8 Oct: €3bn a year), Deutsche Bank (1 Oct: "potentially €12bn") Well known; priced Agreed; no gap
Customers calling Siemens Energy "transactional" Expert calls only (19 Oct 2026 procurement head) Not in sell-side research A 2029-plus share risk; not an 18-month item
800-volt direct current and solid-state transformers Morgan Stanley technology (12, 18, 19 Oct), Jefferies (20 Oct: a $1bn market), Huatai (21 Sep) Specialist research; not in the Siemens Energy debate Agreed it is post-2028
Currency translation Management (every call); Deutsche Bank treats it as a technicality Known; dismissed Agreed for margins; it will trim reported revenue estimates
Grid price-cost on 2028 deliveries Morgan Stanley (26 Oct: Europe plateaued; price-cost a headwind beyond 2030); Ambit (India) One house, unquantified Our second set's grid path is more cautious than any published model

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • The sell-side narrative, in one sentence. It has moved from "turnaround" (2024 to 2025) through "AI-power compounder" (to mid-2026) to "the order peak and what the 2030 target will say" (since August), while estimates kept rising. The same houses that carry Buy ratings (Deutsche Bank, Morgan Stanley, Jefferies, UBS, Goldman, Bernstein, Evercore) write the most about the risks; the two sells (Barclays, Rothschild & Co Redburn) are valuation and capital-cycle calls, not earnings calls. Nobody models a FY2027 earnings miss; everyone models a FY2030-plus margin question.
  • Investor commentary, from the proxies in the files. Morgan Stanley's investor-feedback notes (15 June, 1 September, 2 October 2026) say: about 70% of surveyed investors own the stock; buy-side margin expectations for FY2030 sit above the sell-side's and "any guidance below 20% to 22% could trigger profit-taking"; the 1 September -5% day was about alternative technologies (engines) and the supply-demand debate; investors ask whether on-site gas is a bridge. Positioning is long (buy share 63%; Refinitiv 79% overweight in the first set's data) and the technical momentum rating is D with relative strength at the 17th percentile: a crowded holder base that has been selling into the theme's de-rating. There is no retail or Twitter evidence in the files; the memo should say so rather than infer it.
  • What is known and over-priced. The peak-orders dynamic: the share price has fallen 29% since late June (Morgan Stanley) while FY2027 EPS rose 11% in six months; the implied terminal margin of 8.5% (Morgan Stanley's reverse DCF) is below the company's FY2026 margin. A risk that is three years from the income statement is priced as if it were present. This is the favourable half of the narrative gap and the core of the memo's topic #7: the catalyst is not new information about demand but the passage of two more quarters in which earnings rise and orders do not collapse.
  • What is known and under-priced. The cash normalisation: every house describes it, none has it in FY2027 to FY2028 numbers, and the pool is larger than any published note uses. The first visible evidence (a quarterly working-capital outflow above €1bn; gas book-to-bill below 1.0) could arrive within the window, most likely in FY2028. This is the unfavourable half of the gap, and it is the one dynamic where our view and the narrative will collide inside 18 months.
  • What is not known. The royalty's stop date as a binary (nobody frames it so); the SG 14 question (two incidents, root causes pending, 67% offshore share); the mid-FY2027 restatement for discontinued operations; the US fence rule on 24 December as a grid pricing extender; the larger-than-reported scale of contract-liability growth in nine months. These are small individually, and together they are where the 11 November and February 2027 prints can surprise in either direction.
  • 18 months to three years. The sell-side articulates the FY2029 to FY2030 dynamics better than the 18-month cash dynamics: pricing stabilisation (Morgan Stanley), competitor capacity (Deutsche Bank, Jefferies), the service annuity as the cushion (Jefferies, Bernstein, Morgan Stanley), the Transformation of Industry and royalty uplifts to 22% (Deutsche Bank). Our FY2030 margin of 18.4% constant perimeter sits below Deutsche Bank's 20% to 22% and Morgan Stanley's 22.3% base, and that difference (new-order price erosion from 2028 rather than a plateau) is a view the narrative does not yet hold. It is the second place our view and the narrative will collide, after 11 November sets the target.

For the Consumer (topics #7, #8, #10): the narrative knows the right things in the wrong order. The memo can argue that the over-known risk (peak orders) is already in a 14.3x multiple, that the under-known risk (cash) is the one to watch and hedge in the sizing, and that the two binary inputs (royalty timing; buyback pace) and the one new quality question (SG 14) are where 11 November and the December annual report can move the story. The lifecycle reading is "thesis broadening while the narrative stalls": earnings momentum A, narrative momentum negative, with the narrative's next positive step dependent on the FY2030 number and the next negative step on the February order comparison.

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 25 October 2026, 17:03 to 17:22 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Siemens Energy (ENR-DE): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Prepared 2026-10-25 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Orientation for the Consumer: read this first

Source checks.

  • Both Earnings Reviews (file 07 "latest" and file 08 "earlier") cover the same print: fiscal Q3 FY2026, the quarter to 30 June 2026, reported 5 August 2026. The caution in the brief is cleared: the latest review does cover the period FactSet shows as the last reported (06/2026). No Q4 FY2026 numbers exist yet. FY2026 results and new FY2030 targets are due on 11 November 2026, the single most important dated event in the next six months.
  • Fiscal year ends 30 September. FY2027 = October 2026 to September 2027.
  • Several source figures conflict. I flag each where it matters. The material ones:
  • The Siemens brand royalty is €51m for FY2024 (annual report) against €300m to €400m a year, about 90bps of margin (Jefferies).
  • The Siemens Energy India stake is 16% (Ambit) or 11% (Deutsche Bank).
  • Gas turbine share is 26% (Jefferies) or 28% of 2025 combined-cycle orders (Bernstein).
  • Q3 consensus for Gas Services orders is €8,287m or €8,475m.
  • One Deutsche Bank line says the "underlying" Grid margin is "closer to 12%" after timing effects. That is inconsistent with the segment data: nine-month FY2026 Grid profit of €1,784m implies about 17%. I treat that line as unreliable.
  • In the FactSet per-broker table, the lower block re-labels brokers (for example, "Rothschild & Co Redburn" carries mwb research's numbers). Use only the upper block for broker attribution.
  • EBIT and margin language: the company's KPI is "profit before special items" (EBITA-like). The FactSet consensus "EBIT" on the brokers' basis tracks it closely: FY2026 consensus €5,303m, against 9M actual €3,946m plus a Q4 of about €1.36bn to €1.42bn. I use "margin" to mean margin before special items unless stated.

The spine of the case, in seven judgements. Every answer below hangs off these.

  1. FY2026 to FY2028 earnings are mostly a backlog-conversion and repricing problem, not a demand problem. - The €162bn backlog covers about 95% of FY2026 and 87% of FY2027 revenue (Huatai). - New orders enter at about 20% higher prices than the book they replace. - The Q3 FY2026 segment margins are already at or near the FY2028 targets: Grid 19.9% and Gas Services 17.3%, against 18% to 20% targets for both. - The 18-month forecast risk is therefore execution (capacity ramp, supply chain, wind quality), not orders.
  2. Demand risk (a gas order normalisation in 2027, or the AI build-out cracking at its financing) bites first on three things, and on FY2027 to FY2028 revenue only last. - The multiple. - Cash flow, because customer prepayments stop growing. - FY2029-and-beyond earnings.
  3. Free cash flow is the most cyclical line and the most over-earned. - FY2025 FactSet free cash flow was 2.7x net income. FY2026 will be about 2x (around €8bn pre-tax guided). - Consensus already fades conversion to 1.0x by FY2028. - A fall in book-to-bill below 1 would push free cash flow well below that, because of the €22.3bn of contract liabilities. The precedent is GE Power in 2017 to 2018.
  4. Siemens Gamesa is the swing factor for earnings and the main source of tail risk. - Each 1pp of Gamesa margin is about €110m pre-tax, about 1.5% of FY2027 EPS. - A second warranty wave is the most plausible sudden €1bn-plus hit.
  5. Consensus is already above formal guidance for FY2028. - FY2028 consensus EBIT margin is 16.3%, against the November 2025 Capital Markets Day range of 14% to 16%. - The 11 November FY2030 targets are therefore a test the company must pass. Morgan Stanley expects 20% to 22% against a consensus of 19.8%. Anything below about 20% would read as a disappointment, because buy-side expectations run above the sell-side's.
  6. The shares have de-rated hard while estimates rose. - The price is €118.68, -38% from the €191.66 high and -32% over six months. - Over the same six months FY2027 consensus EPS rose 11%. - The 24-month-forward P/E is 14.3x: the 29th percentile of five years, the lowest of the last two years, and inside the stock's own ten-year "conservative" band of 14.8x.
  7. Three portfolio discontinuities hit the FY2027 to FY2028 model, and consensus probably does not treat them consistently. - The Transformation of Industry deconsolidation, decision about mid-2027. The division had €5.7bn of FY2025 revenue and a margin of about 13% to 14%. - Siemens Energy India: a 51% control stake, fully consolidated by June 2028, for about €4.5bn. - The end of the Siemens brand royalty through the Omterra rebrand, from late 2026.

Inside the group Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)

Answers are given by business where the answer differs. FY2026 consensus revenue mix: Gas Services about 32%, Grid Technologies about 32%, Siemens Gamesa about 24%, Transformation of Industry about 13%. Profit mix is far more concentrated: Grid about 47% and Gas about 40% of FY2026 profit before special items, with wind about 0%.

Inside the group Company - Economic strength (Layer 1)

Basis note: consensus is on the brokers' basis. History growth rates come from FactSet's history lines and must not be mixed with consensus growth (FactSet quirk 1). FY2025 brokers'-basis base: revenue €39.1bn, EBITDA €3.7bn (9.5%), EPS €1.63.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Siemens Energy AG (ENR-DE), a German power-equipment group (to be renamed Omterra) with four segments: Gas Services (gas and steam turbines and their long-term service), Grid Technologies (HVDC, transformers, switchgear, grid systems), Transformation of Industry (compressors, steam turbines, electrolysers; separation under way) and Siemens Gamesa (onshore and offshore wind turbines and service)

Request: T-P1

Peers: GE Vernova (closest listed peer across gas turbines, grid and wind; its Power, Electrification and Wind segments are the direct comparisons); Mitsubishi Heavy Industries (the third member of the large gas turbine oligopoly); Hitachi Ltd for Hitachi Energy (the largest high-voltage grid competitor, with stated margin targets); ABB (electrification peer with a documented multi-year margin expansion programme); Vestas Wind Systems (wind peer with a completed warranty and cost-out turnaround); Nordex (onshore wind peer with an asset-light cost structure); Prysmian (grid cable maker running a capacity expansion while repricing a backlog) Inputs:

  • What share of Siemens Energy's cost base is fixed versus variable in each segment, and what are the main fixed items?
  • Factory overhead.
  • Engineering.
  • Depreciation, about €1.5bn in FY2025 (FactSet).
  • SG&A.
  • R&D, €1.21bn in FY2025 (annual report).
  • How did the margins of Siemens Power and Gas, GE Power and Mitsubishi Heavy Industries' power business move with volume in the 2016 to 2020 large gas turbine downturn? What restructuring did each announce, what did it deliver against what was promised, and over what period?
  • In Gas Services and Grid Technologies over FY2024 to FY2026, how much of the margin expansion (Grid 19.9% and Gas Services 17.3% in Q3 FY2026, against 15.9% and 13.0% a year earlier; company Q3 FY2026 report, 5 August 2026) came from volume absorption, and how much from price and backlog mix?
  • Siemens Gamesa cost programme: the onshore footprint reduction from 10 to 4 factories, the €300m integration savings, and fewer platform variants.
  • What has been delivered so far, and what is the segment's breakeven revenue level?
  • How did Vestas's fixed costs and warranty provisions (6.4% of revenue in 2022 to about 3% in 2026) behave during its recovery?
  • What is the actual annual cost of the Siemens brand licence?
  • The annual report shows €51m for FY2024; Jefferies (14 July 2026) cites €300m in the last year, rising toward €400m in FY2026.
  • In which line does it sit, and when does the Omterra rebrand end it?
  • What is the current SG&A ratio against the FY2028 target of 8.7% of revenue (Capital Markets Day, November 2025), and which cost lines carry the reduction?
  • How did quality-related costs at Siemens Gamesa trend (€450m of quality-related cash outflows in FY2025; earnings presentation), and which non-conformance costs proved cuttable at peers?
  • What stranded corporate costs and separation costs typically follow a carve-out of the size of Transformation of Industry (€5.7bn FY2025 revenue, 17,000 staff)? What did ABB report after selling its Robotics division?
  • What ramp-up inefficiencies have peers incurred when adding large brownfield capacity in transformers, switchgear and turbines?
  • The NKT Karlskrona overrun.
  • Hitachi Energy's US expansion.
  • What does Siemens Energy's plan for 50% more transformer and switchgear capacity by 2030 imply for fixed costs?
  • How did high-voltage grid equipment margins at Siemens, ABB, Alstom Grid and Hitachi respond to the 2010 to 2013 order slowdown? Focus: How much of Siemens Energy's FY2026 to FY2028 margin expansion is volume-driven operating leverage that would reverse in a downturn, against price and backlog mix that is already locked in? Which remaining cost reductions (wind, brand licence, SG&A, separation) are realistic, and in which years would they reach the income statement?

Request: T-P2

Peers: GE Vernova (price setter in large gas turbines; publishes pricing on slot reservation agreements); Mitsubishi Heavy Industries (gas turbine oligopoly member); Hitachi Ltd for Hitachi Energy (grid pricing and capacity); ABB (price-cost pass-through in electrification); Vestas Wind Systems and Nordex (wind turbine pricing discipline against Chinese competition); Goldwind (Chinese wind exporter setting the low-price reference); Prysmian and NKT (backlog repricing in high-voltage cables); HD Hyundai Electric and Hyosung Heavy Industries (Korean transformer makers gaining export share) Operating conditions:

  • Sold-out capacity with multi-year lead times (2024 to 2027).
  • An order downturn after a peak: the large gas turbine downturn of about 2016 to 2020, and the grid slowdown of about 2010 to 2013.
  • Raw material spikes: copper, grain-oriented electrical steel and aluminium in 2021 to 2022 and in 2026.
  • New competitor capacity arriving, such as GE Vernova's plan to reach 30 GW of annual gas turbine output by 2030 (company disclosure, July 2026) and new US transformer plants.
  • Customer financing stress among data-centre developers and offshore wind developers.

  • Large US dollar moves against the euro. Inputs:

  • How much higher is pricing on new Siemens Energy gas turbine orders than on the existing backlog?

  • Deutsche Bank (August 2026) cites about 20% higher pricing year on year, at about €440m per GW.
  • Why was pricing per kW flat sequentially in Q3 FY2026 (Morgan Stanley, 6 August 2026)?
  • Why does Siemens Energy's average gas turbine price per kW differ from GE Vernova's ($572 against $800 per kW; Morgan Stanley, August 2026)? Is the gap mix, geography or pricing power?
  • What happened to large gas turbine prices and service contract terms in the 2016 to 2020 downturn, and how quickly did prices fall after orders peaked?
  • How do Siemens Energy's grid contracts pass through copper and electrical steel costs: indexation formulas, lags and coverage of the backlog? Ambit Capital (22 September 2026) reports flat per-MVA transformer pricing despite a 45% to 50% rise in copper costs.
  • How large are reservation fees and advance payments in Siemens Energy's contracts? Are they refundable? How did customers treat slot reservation agreements when demand softened in past cycles?
  • How do long-term service agreement escalators work in practice (an expert call cites 80% indexation to factory consumer prices and 20% to wages), and how have renewal prices moved?
  • What premium do SF6-free switchgear and HVDC systems command, and does it persist once competitors certify?
  • How did Siemens Gamesa's onshore and offshore turbine prices compare with Vestas and Nordex in 2025 to 2026, and how large is the Chinese price gap in markets where Chinese makers can bid?
  • What do customers (utilities, transmission operators, data-centre developers, offshore developers), competitors and the sell-side say now about Siemens Energy's pricing, terms and responsiveness against Hitachi Energy and GE Vernova? Focus: How much of Siemens Energy's current gas and grid pricing is scarcity rent that will reverse when lead times normalise, and how much is durable (technology, service indexation, regulation)? How long will the repricing of the existing backlog keep lifting reported margins after order growth peaks?

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Siemens Energy's earnings, free cash flow and valuation are governed by interacting loops: customer prepayments funding buybacks, a growing installed base feeding service revenue, scarcity pricing attracting competitor capacity, and wind quality driving market share. The next set needs their timing and strength to model FY2027 to FY2030. Peers: GE Vernova (the same prepayment, slot-reservation and service-annuity model); Mitsubishi Heavy Industries (gas turbine capacity and pricing behaviour); Vestas Wind Systems (contract-asset and warranty loops in wind); Hitachi Ltd for Hitachi Energy (grid capacity response); ABB (backlog conversion and price-cost loops); Prysmian (prepayment-funded working capital in cables); GE as GE Power in 2016 to 2019 (precedent for a service and progress-collection reversal) Inputs:

  • The prepayment loop:
  • Orders above revenue raise contract liabilities (€22.3bn; company reports), which fund cash and buybacks.
  • How does this reverse when book-to-bill falls below 1?
  • What does Bernstein's estimate of a €3bn to €4bn cumulative working-capital outflow by 2028 assume?
  • What happened at GE Power in 2017 to 2019?
  • The installed-base loop:
  • Every turbine shipped adds a long-term service agreement (17-year average; Deutsche Bank).
  • Deutsche Bank estimates 160 GW of additions by 2030 could add €3.6bn of annual service revenue.
  • How fast does service revenue follow unit deliveries, and what share of new units take long-term agreements?
  • The capacity loop: does scarcity pricing in gas turbines and transformers attract capacity that ends the pricing window?
  • GE Vernova to 30 GW by 2030.
  • Hitachi Energy's Mississippi plant by 2029.
  • Korean makers' 19% share of US transformer imports (Morgan Stanley, 27 July 2026).
  • When does the balancing effect reach new-order prices?
  • The customer goodwill loop: do customers who describe Siemens Energy as "transactional" (expert calls, 2026) shift share to Hitachi Energy, ABB or GE Vernova once lead times normalise?
  • The wind loop: quality failures led to onshore share loss, then under-absorption, then margin pressure.
  • Is it now reversing, with Siemens Gamesa's first quarterly profit since 2022 (Q3 FY2026) and first SG 7.0 orders?
  • Or is it continuing, with Q3 FY2026 orders down 77%?
  • The data-centre loop: grid connection queues lead to behind-the-meter gas turbines, which lead to utility affordability pushback (the Ratepayer Protection Act passed the House in September 2026), which slows utility capex. How strong is each step?
  • The reflexive capital-return loop: lower share prices make buybacks more accretive. Does Siemens Energy adjust buyback pace to price? Focus: Which loops will dominate Siemens Energy's free cash flow and margins in FY2027 to FY2029, above all the prepayment and working-capital loop and the competitor-capacity loop? When does each turn from reinforcing to balancing?

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Siemens Energy's FY2027 to FY2028 EPS depends on several below-the-operating-line and portfolio inputs that the sell-side may treat inconsistently, so the next set needs a clean, sourced view of each. Peers: GE Vernova (tax, buybacks, net cash interest and portfolio separations after its own spin-off); ABB (accounting for a large divestment and a deal-contingent hedge); Siemens AG (parent-side treatment of the India and brand transactions); Vestas Wind Systems (warranty provision utilisation and tax); Prysmian (buybacks, minority interests and acquisition accounting) Inputs:

  • Transformation of Industry separation:
  • Expected timing (no decision before mid-calendar 2027; company pre-close call, 30 September 2026).
  • Accounting treatment as a discontinued operation.
  • The retained minority stake and the likely use of proceeds.
  • The reported sale process for the steam turbine business and the named bidders.
  • Siemens Energy India:
  • Terms and timing of the move to a 51% controlling stake (Deutsche Bank cites about €4.5bn and full consolidation by June 2028).
  • The current holding (Ambit Capital and Deutsche Bank give different figures).
  • The effect on revenue, minority interests and cash.
  • The brand licence fee: size and end date (see T-P1).
  • Tax rate: 32.3% reported in FY2025 (FactSet) and 25.4% in the first half of FY2026 (half-year report). What does management guide for FY2027 to FY2028, and what are the drivers?
  • Net interest:
  • Interest income on about €11bn of gross cash at Q3 FY2026.
  • The end of state guarantee fees.
  • Lease and pension interest.
  • Share count:
  • Buybacks under the €6bn programme to FY2028 (about €2.8bn done by end-July 2026).
  • The third €2bn tranche to 31 March 2027.
  • Morgan Stanley's expectation (8 October 2026) of about €3bn a year.
  • Any dilution from employee share plans or past convertible and hybrid instruments.
  • Special items: expected restructuring, separation, rebrand and wind remediation charges in FY2027 to FY2028.
  • Siemens Gamesa warranty provisions (€2,474m) and onerous-contract provisions (€984m) at 30 September 2025: utilisation schedule and release or top-up risk.
  • Currency translation: the US dollar share of revenue and profit, and the translation sensitivity.
  • What does each major broker (Deutsche Bank, Morgan Stanley, Jefferies, Bernstein, UBS, Goldman Sachs, Barclays, mwb research) assume for these items? How far apart are they, and how far from management? Focus: A clean, sourced bridge from Siemens Energy's FY2025 EPS (€1.63 on the brokers' basis, FactSet) to FY2028 that separates operating, portfolio (Transformation of Industry, India, brand licence), financing, tax and share-count effects. For each input, what is management's view against consensus's?

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 26 October 2026, 05:27 to 05:45 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Siemens Energy (ENR-DE): second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Prepared 2026-10-25 for the Investment Analyst answering the third set (predictability of the 18-month forecasts) and the fourth set (how far our view is reflected in guidance, consensus and narrative).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Orientation for the Consumer: read this first

Bases and conventions used in every answer below. Mixing these bases is the single easiest way to misread this company.

  • "EBITA" means the company's own KPI, profit before special items: before special items and before amortisation of intangibles acquired in business combinations. FactSet's consensus "EBIT" is on this basis (FY2026 consensus €5,303m against a nine-month actual of €3,946m plus a fourth quarter of about €1.4bn). FactSet's own "EBIT (adjusted)" history is lower (nine months €3,577m) and must not be used as the base.
  • "EBITDA" means EBITA plus depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired-intangible amortisation. This reproduces the brokers'-basis FY2025 EBITDA exactly: €2,355m EBITA plus €1,359m equals €3,714m (9.5% of revenue). FactSet's quarterly "operating EBITDA" history is after special items and is lower (Q3 FY2026: 16.5% on FactSet's line against 17.5% on this basis).
  • "EPS" means adjusted EPS: before special items and acquired-intangible amortisation, after tax and minorities. Warning for the fourth set: the FY2025 brokers'-basis EPS of €1.63 equals reported basic EPS, and the forward consensus medians mix brokers who adjust and brokers who do not (FY2026 per-broker range €4.11 to €5.26). An adjusted EPS therefore sits about 3% to 5% above the consensus median for definitional reasons alone. Reported EPS runs about €0.45 to €0.70 below adjusted EPS in FY2026 to FY2028.
  • "Free cash flow" (FCF) in my figures is operating cash flow after tax, less capital spending, less lease payments. FactSet's consensus FCF does not deduct lease payments (about €0.45bn a year), and the company's guided "free cash flow pre-tax" (about €8bn for FY2026) deducts neither tax nor leases.
  • Perimeter. I keep Transformation of Industry consolidated in all five years (constant perimeter). The reasons: no structural decision before mid-calendar 2027; the structure (majority sale through Goldman Sachs, spin-off, or a retained minority) is undecided; the earliest plausible closing is in FY2028; consensus almost certainly models it the same way; and the earnings-per-share effect of a sale is close to neutral if proceeds fund buybacks. The deconsolidation overlay is quantified in P-10. Siemens Energy India is consolidated from Q4 FY2028 (51% control by June 2028, about €4.5bn of cash in FY2028), because that step is management's stated plan and its cash outflow matters for buyback capacity.
  • Currency. No translation effect is assumed after FY2026, so reported growth equals comparable growth from FY2027. A 10% move in EUR/USD changes reported revenue by about 3% (Americas 31% and the US 22% of revenue) with little margin effect, because sourcing is about 95% local and transaction exposure is 75% to 100% hedged (net unhedged US dollar exposure €210m).

The five judgements that carry the model.

  1. FY2027 to FY2028 EBITA is close to consensus, but built differently. Revenue is about 2% to 3% below consensus (wind flat, grid capacity-limited), and margins are above it (Gas Services and Grid at the top of their 18% to 20% Capital Markets Day ranges by FY2028). The mix of my errors is therefore more likely to show up in revenue than in profit.
  2. Free cash flow is where I differ most from consensus. My FY2027 and FY2028 FCF of €5.5bn and €5.2bn sit about €0.9bn and €1.1bn below consensus on a like-for-like basis (adding back lease payments of about €0.45bn a year, because FactSet's consensus FCF does not deduct them), and €1.3bn to €1.6bn below it as printed, because the prepayment loop stops adding cash in FY2027 and starts taking cash back from FY2028 (cumulative working-capital outflow of about €3.5bn over FY2028 to FY2030, in line with Bernstein's €3bn to €4bn). Cash conversion falls from 1.7 times adjusted net income in FY2026 to about 0.8 times in FY2028 to FY2030.
  3. The FY2026 to FY2028 margin step is mostly locked in. About half of the €3.8bn EBITA increase is volume on the existing backlog at today's margins; about one-third is price and mix already in the backlog plus fixed-cost absorption; about one-sixth is self-help (wind repair and the end of the brand royalty). Only the absorption part, about €0.5bn to €0.7bn, would reverse quickly in a downturn.
  4. FY2029 to FY2030 sit below consensus and below the FY2030 targets the market expects. My FY2030 group margin is 18.4% on a constant perimeter and about 18.9% excluding Transformation of Industry, against the 20% to 22% Morgan Stanley and Deutsche Bank expect management to target on 11 November. The gap comes from a grid price-cost squeeze on FY2028 to FY2029 deliveries and from the end of the backlog repricing step, not from a demand collapse.
  5. The single largest unresolved input is the Siemens brand royalty. I assume a saving of €100m in FY2027 and about €250m a year from FY2028. The evidence spans €51m (FY2024 annual report, at a 1% margin) to €400m (Jefferies, FY2026, under a margin-linked fee of 0.3% to 1.2% of revenue). The difference is worth about 0.5pp of FY2028 margin and about 3% of FY2028 EPS. It should be resolved by the FY2026 annual report and the 11 November presentation.

Where I disagree with the first set.

  • The first set expected the share count to fall about 3% a year. With buyback prices rising as earnings rise, €3bn a year retires about 2.5% a year (FY2025 855m shares to an FY2030 average of 768m).
  • The first set judged that consensus FCF fading to 1.0 times net income by FY2028 "already assumes neutral working capital". I do not think so: consensus FY2027 to FY2028 FCF of €6.8bn a year, with consensus capital spending falling to €1.86bn, still embeds either a working-capital inflow or light capital spending. My FCF is lower on both counts.
  • The first set left the brand royalty unresolved between €51m and €400m. I take a middle value (judgement 5), because the €51m figure belongs to a year when the margin-linked fee was at its floor and cannot describe a year at a 12% margin.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Three cautions for the Financial Analyst before using Parts 1 to 3.

  1. Working capital is on the company's net working capital basis, with contract liabilities netted, not on FactSet's "receivables plus inventories less payables" line. FactSet's line (41.1% of revenue at FY2025, 46.4% at Q3 FY2026) leaves out €22.3bn of contract liabilities and would show this company as absorbing cash when it is generating it. My FY2025 opening balance on the company basis is about -14.1% of revenue (about -€5.5bn; an estimate, uncertain by about ±€1.5bn, because the company's definition and the Bernstein "-78 days" figure do not reconcile exactly). If the model takes its FY2025 opening from the FactSet line, replace it with -14.1%, or FY2026 cash flow will be misstated by about €20bn. What matters is the change: my working-capital cash flows are +€3.6bn (FY2026), +€1.0bn, -€0.8bn, -€1.5bn and -€1.2bn (FY2030). I fold the use of Siemens Gamesa's warranty and onerous-contract provisions (about €0.4bn to €0.5bn a year) into these flows.
  2. EBITDA is before special items (company profit before special items plus depreciation excluding acquired-intangible amortisation). This basis reproduces the brokers' FY2025 EBITDA of €3,714m (9.5%). FactSet's quarterly "operating EBITDA" history is after special items and lower (Q3 FY2026 16.5% against 17.5% on this basis), so Part 3 should not be compared with that history line.
  3. The inputs have no minority line. Minorities are about €45m to €75m a year to FY2028 and about €155m to €175m in FY2029 to FY2030 once Siemens Energy India is consolidated (49% outside shareholders). Without them the model's EPS will be about €0.05 to €0.09 too high to FY2028 and about €0.20 to €0.23 too high in FY2029 to FY2030.

Reference points: Q3 FY2026 margins were Gas Services 17.3%, Grid 19.9%, Transformation of Industry 14.3% and Siemens Gamesa 2.7%; nine-month FY2026 profit before special items was Gas Services €1,715m, Grid €1,784m, Transformation of Industry €543m, Siemens Gamesa -€15m and group €3,946m (12.6%). Perimeter: Transformation of Industry is kept consolidated in all years (constant perimeter; no structural decision before mid-2027 and the structure is undecided). The deconsolidation overlay: revenue about -€6.6bn a year, EBITA about -€0.9bn plus about €0.1bn of stranded cost, group margin about +0.1pp to +0.3pp, EPS about -1% to -2% if net proceeds of about €8bn fund buybacks, about -5% if held in cash.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue. Capacity- and price-limited, not demand-limited: the €162bn backlog covers about 95% of FY2026 and 87% of FY2027 revenue. Large gas turbine output rises from 35 to 50 units in FY2027 and medium units from 50 to 80 and towards 100 by FY2028; transformer and switchgear capacity rises 50% by 2030 (Charlotte expansion early 2027). Realised price adds about 5pp to growth in FY2027, 4.5pp in FY2028, 3pp in FY2029 and 2pp in FY2030. Siemens Gamesa is flat (Q3 FY2026 book-to-bill 0.4; selective bidding; offshore awards slipped into FY2027). No currency translation is assumed after FY2026. Siemens Energy India adds about €0.3bn in FY2028 and €1.3bn to €1.5bn a year after.
  • Q4 FY2026. Revenue about €12.35bn (+18.4%, against +17.5% in Q3) and profit before special items about €1.41bn (11.4%), below Q3's 14.2% because Q3 carried project one-offs and management warned of Q4 seasonality. FY2026 margin before special items 12.2%, against guidance of "the upper end of 10% to 12%".
  • Quarterly phasing, FY2027. Growth is higher in H1 (+15% and +16%) because Q1 and Q2 FY2026 were depressed by the weak dollar (reported +8.2% and +3.3%), and lower in H2 (+11%) against tougher comparisons. EBITDA margins follow the usual seasonality, with Q3 the strongest.
  • Margins. Backlog repricing (new gas orders about 20% above backlog pricing; grid backlog margin rising about 300bps a year), service growth on a growing fleet (over 70% long-term agreement attach rate, 17-year contracts), wind repair, the end of the brand royalty, and overheads growing about 7% a year against revenue at 12% to 13% (selling, general and administrative costs from 8.9% to about 8.0% of revenue by FY2028). Incremental EBITA margins of 28% to 34%. From FY2029 the margin gain slows: the repricing step is used up and a grid price-cost squeeze (flat per-MVA transformer prices against copper +45% to 50%, Ambit) is allowed for.
  • Depreciation. Rises with the €6bn FY2026 to FY2028 capacity programme, from about 3.5% to about 3.2% of revenue.
  • Net financial items. Interest income on €10bn to €11bn of gross cash at about 3%, less about €190m of bond, loan and lease interest and about €20m of pension interest; guarantee fees have ended. Lower from FY2028 after the India payment.
  • Tax. P&L rate 25.5% rising to 27% as Siemens Gamesa's loss carry-forwards are used; cash tax rate guided "slightly shy of 20%" for FY2026 to FY2028, which I use in my FCF (17%, 19%, 20%, then 23% and 25%). If the model applies the P&L rate to cash tax, its FCF will be about €0.25bn to €0.6bn a year lower than mine.
  • Capital spending. €2.0bn (FY2026; nine months €1.125bn), €2.2bn, €2.3bn, then €2.2bn: above consensus (€2.18bn, €2.05bn, €1.86bn, €1.71bn) because grid capacity rises 50% to 2030 and blade and vane integration continues.
  • Working capital. Contract liabilities still grow in FY2027 (book-to-bill about 1.15 to 1.2), then normalise as the FY2025 to FY2026 gas prepayments convert into deliveries; cumulative outflow of about €3.5bn over FY2028 to FY2030, in line with Bernstein's €3bn to €4bn.
  • Acquisitions. Siemens Energy India 51% control by June 2028 for about €4.5bn (Deutsche Bank; could move with the two Indian share prices), plus about €0.1bn a year of small vertical deals (castings, copper).
  • Buybacks. €3.05bn in FY2026 (tranches one and two plus the start of tranche three), then €3bn a year, assuming the pace is raised from about €2bn to about €3bn a year on 11 November (Morgan Stanley's expectation). My assumed buyback prices: about €130 (FY2027), €150, €165, €180. Shares delivered to employee plans from treasury add about 1m shares a year; no convertible or other dilutive instrument remains.
  • Dividends. €1.90 for FY2026 (about 45% of reported profit), then about 40% of adjusted EPS, inside the 40% to 60% policy.

I expect the initial FY2027 guide to sit below my figures, in line with the company's habit of guiding low and raising

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Operating profit. The company's KPI, profit before special items, is EBITA before special items and before amortisation of intangibles acquired in business combinations. FactSet's consensus "EBIT" (FY2026 €5,303m) is on this basis. FactSet's "EBIT (adjusted)" history is lower (nine months FY2026 €3,577m against €3,946m), the difference being special items and acquired-intangible amortisation.
  • EBITDA. Brokers' basis equals profit before special items plus depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired intangibles: FY2025 €2,355m + €1,359m = €3,714m (9.5%). FactSet's operating EBITDA (€3,513m, 9.0%) is after special items.
  • Net income and EPS. Adjusted EPS = (profit before special items + net financial items) x (1 - tax rate) - minorities, over weighted average shares. Reported EPS also deducts special items (about €0.35bn, €0.5bn, €0.4bn, €0.2bn, €0.15bn pre-tax in FY2026 to FY2030) and acquired-intangible amortisation (about €0.25bn a year); my reported EPS estimates are €4.26, €5.91, €7.76, €9.50 and €10.91. The FY2025 brokers'-basis EPS of €1.63 equals reported basic EPS, while FactSet's adjusted diluted EPS for FY2025 is €1.93. The forward consensus median mixes adjusted and reported definitions (FY2026 per-broker range €4.11 to €5.26; the lower figures, for example Metzler €4.11 and Barclays €4.26, are close to my reported estimate). So my adjusted EPS sits about 3% to 5% above the consensus median in FY2026 to FY2027 partly for definitional reasons.
  • Free cash flow. The company guides "free cash flow pre-tax": operating cash flow before income taxes, less capital spending; lease payments are not deducted. FactSet's FCF (and its consensus, €7,615m for FY2026) is operating cash flow after tax less capital spending, also before lease payments. My FCF deducts tax and lease payments, so it is about €0.45bn a year below a like-for-like FactSet figure. On a like-for-like basis my FCF is about €0.3bn below consensus in FY2026, about €0.9bn below in FY2027 and about €1.1bn below in FY2028.
  • Net cash. FactSet's debt includes about €2.1bn of lease liabilities; the company's adjusted net cash (about €7.7bn at Q3 FY2026) and FactSet's (€8.1bn) differ for this and other reasons.
  • Currency. Reported revenue includes translation (-560bps in Q2 FY2026, -50bps in Q3); consensus revenue is reported, not comparable. My inputs assume no translation effect after FY2026.

Your cross-check: your own earnings per share and free cash flow for each of the five fiscal years

Earnings per share is adjusted (before special items and acquired-intangible amortisation, after minorities), in EUR. Free cash flow is after tax and after lease payments, before acquisitions, dividends and buybacks, in EUR millions.

Earnings per share 4.71 6.58 8.36 9.91 11.27

How the cross-check is built: EBITA €5,356m, €7,289m, €9,180m, €10,838m, €12,056m; plus net financial items; less tax at the Part 1 rates; less minorities of €45m, €50m, €73m, €154m, €174m; over average shares of 845m, 823m, 802m, 784m, 768m. FCF = EBITDA, less special-item cash (€350m, €500m, €400m, €200m, €150m), plus net financial items, less cash tax (17%, 19%, 20%, 23%, 25% of pre-tax profit), less capital spending, plus the working-capital flow, less lease payments. Where the model's figures differ from these, the likely causes are, in order: the working-capital opening balance (caution 1), minorities (caution 3), cash tax against P&L tax, special-item cash, and the buyback price the model assumes.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Siemens Energy AG (ENR-DE), a German power-equipment group (to be renamed Omterra) with four segments: Gas Services (gas and steam turbines and their long-term service), Grid Technologies (HVDC systems, transformers, switchgear, grid systems), Transformation of Industry (compressors, steam turbines, electrolysers; legal separation under way) and Siemens Gamesa (onshore and offshore wind turbines and service)

Request: T-D1

Peers: GE Vernova (closest listed peer in gas turbines, grid and wind, running the same slot-reservation, prepayment and service model); Mitsubishi Heavy Industries (third member of the large gas turbine oligopoly, adding capacity more cautiously); Hitachi Ltd for Hitachi Energy (largest high-voltage grid competitor, expanding US transformer capacity); ABB (electrification peer with a documented multi-year margin expansion from 2019 to 2026); Vestas Wind Systems (wind peer that completed a warranty and margin turnaround from 2022 to 2026); Nordex (onshore wind peer that took European share during Siemens Gamesa's quality crisis); Prysmian and NKT (high-voltage cable makers ramping new capacity while repricing their backlogs) Operating conditions: sold-out factory capacity with multi-year lead times (2024 to 2027); a large capacity ramp under way at the same time as a portfolio separation, a rebrand and an acquisition; an order peak followed by normalisation (as in large gas turbines after 2015 and high-voltage grid after 2010); rising interest rates and credit stress among data-centre and offshore wind developers; a wind turnaround after a quality crisis Inputs: Can Siemens Energy deliver its November 2025 Capital Markets Day targets for FY2028 (group margin before special items of 14% to 16%; Gas Services and Grid Technologies 18% to 20%; Siemens Gamesa 3% to 5%; low-teens revenue growth), and whatever FY2027 guidance and FY2030 targets it set at its FY2026 results on 11 November 2026, on the strengths it already has rather than on strengths it would need to acquire? Test the demonstrated strengths: a €162bn order backlog (Q3 FY2026 report); new gas turbine orders priced about 20% above a year earlier (Deutsche Bank, August 2026); Grid Technologies margin of 19.9% and Gas Services 17.3% in Q3 FY2026; a long-term service base with 17-year average contracts; net cash of about €8bn. Test the strengths it still has to prove: raising large gas turbine output from 35 to 50 units in FY2027 and medium units from 50 to 80 and towards 100 (company, September 2026), and transformer and switchgear capacity by 50% by 2030, without ramp-up inefficiency (precedent: NKT's Karlskrona overrun); sustaining Siemens Gamesa's profit after its first profitable quarter since 2022 (€75m in Q3 FY2026) with orders down 77% in that quarter and €2,474m of warranty provisions outstanding; securing hot-section castings, grain-oriented electrical steel, bushings and skilled high-voltage labour; executing the Transformation of Industry separation, the Omterra rebrand and the Siemens Energy India consolidation at the same time. What is the record of the current management (in office since 2020) on capacity ramps, wind quality and guidance, against peers? Focus: Whether Siemens Energy's margin path to FY2028 and beyond rests only on conversion of an already priced backlog and on capacity it is already installing, or also on a wind turnaround and a supply-chain performance it has not yet demonstrated; and what the precedents say about how often companies in this position delivered such a margin step on time.

Request: T-D2

Peers: GE Vernova (same business model; its 2026 to 2028 results show how backlog repricing and prepayments flow through); Mitsubishi Heavy Industries (gas turbine backlog conversion and capacity behaviour); Hitachi Ltd for Hitachi Energy (grid margin trajectory and capacity additions); ABB (price-cost pass-through and backlog conversion in electrification); Vestas Wind Systems (warranty provisions and contract-asset dynamics in wind); Prysmian (prepayment-funded working capital in high-voltage cables); GE as GE Power in 2016 to 2019 (precedent for a progress-collection and service reversal) Operating conditions: an order peak in gas turbines in 2026 followed by possible normalisation in 2027; competitor capacity arriving in 2027 to 2030 (GE Vernova to 30 GW of gas turbines a year by 2030; Hitachi Energy's Mississippi transformer plant in 2029); copper, steel and aluminium price spikes against flat transformer prices; US utility affordability pressure on capital spending; large EUR/USD moves; a portfolio separation and an acquisition changing the perimeter Inputs: Which few drivers separate Siemens Energy's FY2027 to FY2030 financials from FY2020 to FY2025, and how concentrated are they? Test: (1) backlog repricing in Gas Services and Grid Technologies and how long it keeps lifting reported margins after order growth peaks; (2) the conversion of contract liabilities (€22.3bn) and reservation fees into cash, and the working-capital outflow if book-to-bill falls towards 1 (Bernstein estimated €3bn to €4bn of cumulative outflow by 2028; Morgan Stanley's bear case, October 2026, a full €16.2bn reversal); (3) Siemens Gamesa's margin and the risk of warranty provision top-ups; (4) the end of the Siemens brand royalty (€51m in the FY2024 annual report; about €300m in FY2025 and €400m in FY2026 according to Jefferies, July 2026); (5) the Transformation of Industry deconsolidation and the Siemens Energy India consolidation (about €4.5bn for a 51% stake by June 2028, Deutsche Bank); (6) currency translation (reported revenue growth 560bps below comparable growth in Q2 FY2026); (7) grid price-cost on deliveries in FY2028 to FY2029. How wide is the dispersion of sell-side forecasts (FactSet FY2028 EPS from €6.56 at mwb research to €9.50 at Barclays, October 2026) and which assumptions explain it? How did outcomes disperse for GE Power, Siemens Power and Gas and the high-voltage grid makers after their order peaks, and how often were forecasts resting on backlog conversion met? Focus: How wide and how skewed the range of outcomes is over the next 18 months for Siemens Energy's EBITA, free cash flow and EPS separately, and whether free cash flow, rather than profit, is where the dispersion is concentrated.

Request: T-I1

Peers: GE Vernova (US bellwether; its Q3 2026 results and 2026 to 2028 guidance set the read-across on gas pricing and capacity); Mitsubishi Heavy Industries (gas turbine orders and forecasts raised in 2026); Hitachi Ltd for Hitachi Energy (grid margin targets and order momentum); ABB (Q3 2026 electrification results and pricing); Schneider Electric (data-centre electrification demand); Vestas Wind Systems and Nordex (wind order and margin read-across); Prysmian and NKT (high-voltage backlog repricing) Operating conditions: an order peak and possible normalisation in gas turbines; sold-out grid capacity with pricing on a plateau; credit stress among data-centre developers and neoclouds; US utility affordability politics (the Ratepayer Protection Act passed the House in September 2026); a risk-off market with a US 10-year yield above 5%; currency translation from a weak or strong US dollar Inputs: What did Siemens Energy guide on 11 November 2026 for FY2027 (revenue growth, margin before special items by segment, free cash flow), what FY2030 targets and capital-return plans did it set, and how did these compare with consensus before and after the event (FactSet consensus FY2028 EBIT margin 16.3% against the 14% to 16% Capital Markets Day range; Morgan Stanley expected FY2030 targets of 20% to 22%, consensus 19.8%)? What is the company's record of guiding low and raising (FY2025 revenue guidance raised twice from 8% to 10% to 13% to 15%; FY2026 free cash flow guidance raised from €4bn to €5bn to about €8bn)? Where does consensus sit against the guidance and against the Q3 FY2026 run-rate (14.2% group margin, flattered by project one-offs)? How are sell-side revisions moving after the results by line (orders, revenue, margin, free cash flow, buybacks), and which brokers moved most? What did peers' Q3 2026 results and guidance imply for gas turbine pricing, grid pricing and wind orders in FY2027? Focus: Whether Siemens Energy's FY2027 guidance and FY2030 targets leave room for raises at the Q1 to Q4 FY2027 results, and whether the next consensus revisions are more likely to be upgrades to margins with cuts to free cash flow and wind, or the reverse.

Request: T-I2

Peers: GE Vernova (the stock investors use as the AI-power bellwether); Mitsubishi Heavy Industries (gas turbine peer); Hitachi Ltd for Hitachi Energy (grid peer); ABB and Schneider Electric (electrification leaders valued as compounders); Vestas Wind Systems and Nordex (wind peers); Prysmian and NKT (cable makers in the same "contracted margin" theme) Operating conditions: a de-rating of AI-power equipment stocks in a risk-off market while earnings estimates rise; a debate about peak gas turbine orders in 2027; competitor capacity additions read as a 2030s oversupply risk; activist pressure for a wind separation; portfolio events (separation, rebrand, acquisition) under way Inputs: the backlog repricing that keeps margins rising after prices plateau; the peak in gas turbine orders and what it means for prepayments and free cash flow; the normalisation of working capital and contract liabilities; GE Vernova's capacity raise to 30 GW and the risk of a 2030s price war; whether behind-the-meter gas for data centres is a bridge or permanent; US utility affordability politics and capex trims; Siemens Gamesa's turnaround and quality risk; the size and timing of the end of the Siemens brand royalty; the Transformation of Industry separation and its value; the Siemens Energy India purchase and minority concerns (Ambit Capital); buyback pace and whether it rises at the 11 November 2026 results; customers describing Siemens Energy as "transactional"; 800-volt direct-current data-centre architecture and solid-state transformers; currency translation Focus: Which of these dynamics investors who own or cover Siemens Energy already price, especially the gas order peak and the free-cash-flow normalisation, and which are discussed by few or none; in particular, whether the size of the brand royalty, the timing of any working-capital reversal and grid price-cost on future deliveries are discussed at all, and by whom.

Request: OWN-1

Question: What is the composition of Siemens Energy's contract liabilities (€22.3bn at Q3 FY2026) and net working capital by segment, how much is customer advance payments, reservation fees and milestone billing, how are these expected to unwind as the gas turbines booked in FY2025 to FY2026 are delivered, and what did management say at the FY2026 results on 11 November 2026 about cash conversion and working capital for FY2027 to FY2028?

Request: OWN-2

Question: What is the actual annual cost of the Siemens brand licence to Siemens Energy in FY2025 and FY2026 (the FY2026 annual report, Jefferies' July 2026 note and the FY2024 annual report figure of €51m), in which line it sits, how the margin-linked fee is calculated, on which revenue base, when exactly payments end under the Omterra rebrand, what one-off rebrand or termination costs apply, and how far brokers' forecasts include the saving?

Request: OWN-3

Question: What is the status of Siemens Gamesa's turnaround after Q4 FY2026: full-year FY2026 profit, the FY2027 order pipeline including the large offshore orders that slipped from FY2026, SG 7.0-170 onshore order traction, the utilisation schedule and adequacy of the €2,474m of warranty provisions and €984m of onerous-contract provisions, any new quality issues after the August 2026 Taiwan offshore turbine fire, and the status of the Ananym Capital campaign for a wind separation?

Request: OWN-4

Request: OWN-5
